[PATCH] KVM: coalesced_mmio: Sanity check the size of writes
Author: Wenmei Wang <wangwenmei0618@163.com>

Each ring entry has a fixed 8-byte data buffer, but the write path only
checks that the access falls within the zone, not that it fits in the
buffer.  All in-tree users of the MMIO bus cap accesses at 8 bytes, so
this cannot be triggered today.  Reject oversized writes anyway instead
of trusting every caller to uphold that limit.

Found by reading the code.

Signed-off-by: Wenmei Wang <wangwenmei0618@163.com>
---
 virt/kvm/coalesced_mmio.c | 3 +++

[PATCH v5] x86/virt/tdx: Gate TDH.SYS.DISABLE on X86_FEATURE_TDX_HOST_PLATFORM
Author: Challvy Tee <challvy.tee@gmail.com>

Commit 5b25f249be32 ("x86/tdx: Disable the TDX module during kexec and
kdump") made native_machine_crash_shutdown() call tdx_sys_disable(),
whose only gate is compile time (CONFIG_INTEL_TDX_HOST).  A kernel that
never initialized the TDX module therefore still issues
TDH.SYS.DISABLE.

In VMX non-root operation on a SEAM-capable host, SEAMCALL causes a VM
exit instead of a native #UD.  KVM injects #UD only since
commit 9d7dfb95da2c ("KVM: VMX: Inject #UD if guest tries to execute
SEAMCALL or TDCALL"), first released in v6.18.  On an older host the
VMM gets KVM_EXIT_INTERNAL_ERROR and the vCPU never resumes, so
machine_kexec() is never reached and no vmcore is written.  Kdump is
thus broken only in guests on SEAM-capable hosts whose hypervisor does
not handle the exit; plain kexec is unaffected because tdx_shutdown()
only runs as a syscore op, registered after init_tdx_module() succeeds.

Gate the SEAMCALL on X86_FEATURE_TDX_HOST_PLATFORM before issuing it,
the way paddr_is_tdx_private() already guards its own SEAMCALL in this
file.  Without that feature the module is never initialized, so there
is nothing to disable; on a TDX host the gate is a no-op.

It also decouples kdump from the host KVM version -- a guest cannot
require its host to inject the #UD.

Fixes: 5b25f249be32 ("x86/tdx: Disable the TDX module during kexec and kdump")
Suggested-by: Kiryl Shutsemau <kas@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Rick Edgecombe <rick.p.edgecombe@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Challvy Tee <challvy.tee@gmail.com>
---

[PATCH v2 10/20] x86/sev: Use non-instrumented bitops in the GHCB accessors
Author: Sasha Levin <sashal@kernel.org>

The GHCB accessors are used from noinstr code, such as
__sev_es_nmi_complete(), but use test_bit() and __set_bit(), which
call the KASAN and KCSAN checks. arch/x86/coco/sev/Makefile disables
KASAN and KCSAN for noinstr.o, which turns those checks into empty
stubs, but they still have to be inlined. Instrumentation such as gcov
can prevent that and leave calls to kasan_check_write() behind in
noinstr code.

Use the arch_ bitops, which aren't instrumented, as is done elsewhere
for noinstr code.

Signed-off-by: Sasha Levin <sashal@kernel.org>
---
 arch/x86/include/asm/svm.h | 8 ++++----
 1 file changed, 4 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

Author: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

[PATCH v2 2/5] vfio: selftests: Skip tests when the IOMMU mode is not available
Author: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

Skip tests when the IOMMU mode they need is not available on the current
system, instead of failing. Add iommu_mode_is_available() to check whether
a given IOMMU mode (or, if NULL, any IOMMU mode) is available,
iommu_mode_unavailable_msg() to explain why a test is skipping, and
SKIP_IF_IOMMU_MODE_UNAVAILABLE() to skip a kselftest harness test if the
mode is not available.

On a kernel without iommufd, for example, all of the iommufd and
iommufd_compat test variants currently fail on an assert in iommu_init().
Skip them instead, with a message that explains why:

  ok 33 vfio_pci_driver_test.iommufd.send_msi # SKIP IOMMU mode 'iommufd' is not available

Use SKIP_IF_IOMMU_MODE_UNAVAILABLE() in all tests that run across multiple
IOMMU modes, and in the tests that let the library pick the mode by
passing NULL to iommu_init(). Also skip vfio_pci_driver_test, which calls
iommu_init() from main() to probe the device, if no IOMMU mode is
available.

Skip from the test via the kselftest harness's SKIP(), rather than from
within iommu_init() via ksft_exit_skip(). ksft_exit_skip() is not aware of
the harness, and so would emit a bogus "1..0 # SKIP" plan line in the
middle of the TAP output, and the harness would report the reason for the
skip as "unknown".

Signed-off-by: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

[PATCH v2 1/5] vfio: selftests: Automatically detect the IOMMU mode
Author: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

Automatically detect the IOMMU mode that is most likely to work on the
current system when iommu_init() is passed a NULL mode, rather than always
using iommufd. Drop the global default_iommu_mode and convert its users to
pass NULL to iommu_init().

iommufd is an optional feature, and VFIO's iommufd support additionally
requires CONFIG_VFIO_DEVICE_CDEV. Hardcoding iommufd as the default causes
tests to fail with an unhelpful assert on otherwise functional kernels
that only support the legacy VFIO container:

  tools/testing/selftests/vfio/lib/iommu.c:448: Assertion Failure

  Expression: iommu->iommufd > 0
  Observed: 0xffffffffffffffff > 0
  [errno: 2 - No such file or directory]

Order iommu_modes[] by preference and pick the first mode that is
available. Consider a mode available if its container (or /dev/iommu) can
be opened and, for container modes, if VFIO_CHECK_EXTENSION reports
support for the mode's IOMMU type. iommufd mode additionally requires VFIO
device cdevs. If no mode is available, fail with a message that explains
why.

Suggested-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/kvm/aiIG2L_KdPwuxYA1@google.com/
Signed-off-by: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

[PATCH v2 0/5] vfio: selftests: Automatically detect the IOMMU mode
Author: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

This series makes the VFIO selftests library automatically detect which
IOMMU mode to use, rather than hardcoding iommufd as the default, and
makes tests skip, rather than fail, when the IOMMU mode they need is not
available. This was suggested by Sean while reviewing the KVM selftest
irq_test, which currently has to probe for the IOMMU mode itself [1].

iommufd is an optional feature, and VFIO's iommufd support additionally
requires CONFIG_VFIO_DEVICE_CDEV. On otherwise functional kernels that
only support the legacy VFIO container, the VFIO selftests (and
irq_test, when using a VFIO device) currently fail with an unhelpful
assert:

  tools/testing/selftests/vfio/lib/iommu.c:448: Assertion Failure

  Expression: iommu->iommufd > 0
  Observed: 0xffffffffffffffff > 0
  [errno: 2 - No such file or directory]

Patch 1 makes iommu_init(NULL) pick the most preferred IOMMU mode that is
available on the current system, and drops the global default_iommu_mode.
iommu_modes[] is now ordered by preference: iommufd, vfio_type1v2_iommu,
vfio_type1_iommu, iommufd_compat_type1v2, iommufd_compat_type1. A mode is
considered available if its container (or /dev/iommu) can be opened and,
for container modes, if VFIO_CHECK_EXTENSION reports support for the
mode's IOMMU type. iommufd mode additionally requires VFIO device cdevs.

Patch 2 adds iommu_mode_is_available(), iommu_mode_unavailable_msg(),
and SKIP_IF_IOMMU_MODE_UNAVAILABLE(), and uses them to skip tests when the
IOMMU mode they need is not available. Tests skip via the kselftest
harness's SKIP() rather than iommu_init() calling ksft_exit_skip(), since
the latter produces malformed TAP output when called from within a
harness test.

Patch 3 makes the VFIO selftests library print the names of all known
IOMMU modes when passed an unrecognized mode, and exposes the list via
iommu_mode_names().

Patch 4 converts irq_test to use the VFIO selftests library to pick the
IOMMU mode, and drops its probe_iommu_type().

Patch 5 lists the known IOMMU modes in irq_test's help output.

This series is based on vfio/next (vfio-v7.4-rc1).

v2:
 - Print the known IOMMU modes when passed an unrecognized mode (patch 3)
   [Sean]
 - Print a helpful message when irq_test skips because the IOMMU mode
   is not available (patch 4) [Sean]
 - List the known IOMMU modes in irq_test's help output (patch 5)
 - Add iommu_mode_unavailable_msg() so that the VFIO selftests and
   irq_test print the same message when skipping, including "No IOMMU
   mode is available. Is VFIO (or IOMMUFD) loaded and enabled?" when no
   IOMMU mode is available (patches 1, 2, and 4)

v1: https://lore.kernel.org/kvm/20261029221710.1871112-1-dmatlack@google.com/

[1] https://lore.kernel.org/kvm/aiIG2L_KdPwuxYA1@google.com/

Testing
-----------------------------------------------------------------------

Tested in a QEMU VM with an emulated igb device behind an emulated Intel
IOMMU, bound to vfio-pci, on a kernel with CONFIG_IOMMUFD=y,
CONFIG_VFIO_DEVICE_CDEV=y, and CONFIG_VFIO_CONTAINER=y. To simulate
kernels with fewer IOMMU modes available, /dev/iommu and /dev/vfio/vfio
were deleted in the VM:

 - All IOMMU modes available: All VFIO selftests pass. irq_test -d passes
   using iommufd. irq_test -d -t bogus fails and lists the known modes.

 - Only the legacy VFIO container available (/dev/iommu deleted): All
   VFIO selftests pass, with the iommufd and iommufd_compat variants
   skipped instead of failing. irq_test -d passes using
   vfio_type1v2_iommu, and skips if -t iommufd is passed.

 - No IOMMU mode available (/dev/iommu and /dev/vfio/vfio deleted):
   vfio_pci_device_test, vfio_pci_driver_test, and irq_test -d all skip
   with a message explaining why, and with well-formed TAP output.

David Matlack (5):
  vfio: selftests: Automatically detect the IOMMU mode in iommu_init()
  vfio: selftests: Skip tests when the IOMMU mode is not available
  vfio: selftests: Print the known IOMMU modes on an unrecognized mode
  KVM: selftests: Use VFIO selftests library to pick IOMMU mode in
    irq_test
  KVM: selftests: List the known IOMMU modes in irq_test's help output

[PATCH v2 4/5] KVM: selftests: Use VFIO selftests library to pick
Author: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

Use the VFIO selftests library to pick the IOMMU mode in irq_test and drop
probe_iommu_type(). Pass NULL to iommu_init() when the user does not
specify a mode with -t, and skip the test if the requested mode (or, if
NULL, any mode) is not available.

The VFIO selftests library now automatically picks the IOMMU mode that is
most likely to work on the current system, so there is no need for each
test to probe for a mode itself.

[PATCH v2 3/5] vfio: selftests: Print the known IOMMU modes on an
Author: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>

Print the names of all known IOMMU modes when iommu_init() is passed an
unrecognized IOMMU mode, and expose the list of names via
iommu_mode_names() so that tests can also print it, e.g. in their help
output.

Users can specify the IOMMU mode for some tests on the command line (e.g.

the valid options are rather than leaving them to go read the library.

[PATCH 7/7] KVM: x86: Flush guest TLB when emulating a RESET to strictly honor the spec
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Flush the guest TLB when emulating a RESET even though such a flush should
be completely unnecessary since KVM only performs a RESET when creating a
vCPU, and so it should be impossible for the vCPU to have any accrued TLB
state.  Making the flush unconditional aligns KVM's behavior with both the
SDM and APM (ignoring that both specifications contradict themselves), and
avoids having to worry about hypotheticals like "what if KVM did provide a
way to emulate RESET after a vCPU is created?".

Signed-off-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Message-ID: <20261022015407.279870-3-seanjc@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c | 21 +++++++++------------
 1 file changed, 9 insertions(+), 12 deletions(-)

[PATCH 6/7] KVM: x86: Unconditionally reset MMU context when emulating INIT
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Force an MMU reset when emulating an INIT even if paging was already
disabled, as the MMU page role includes guest_mode (L2), i.e. an INIT while
L2 is running with CR0.PG=0 will result in KVM running L1 with a stale MMU
context.

Alternatively, the reset could be scoped strictly to paging being enabled
or L2 being active, but SMM is also encoded in the page role.  Even though
it _should_ be impossible for the vCPU to be INIT'd while SMM is active,
there's zero value in not handling SMM.  At that point, the only scenario
where KVM _doesn't_ need to reset the MMU context is on an INIT with the
vCPU running in Real Mode or Unpaged Protect Mode, and while INIT in those
modes is somewhat common, it's far from a hot path and re-initializing the
MMU is not expensive, especially when the role isn't changing (KVM elides
most of the re-configuration in that case).

Note, like the Fixes commit, this bug only affects nested SVM as INIT is an
unconditional intercept on VMX, i.e. it's impossible to emulate INIT while
L2 is active.

Temporarily keep the guest TLB flush when CR0.PG is being cleared even
though it is fully redundant with the flush that is emitted on all INIT
events (KVM itself only does RESET when a vCPU is being created, i.e. when
CR0.PG must be '0').

Fixes: ed129ec9057f ("KVM: x86: forcibly leave nested mode on vCPU reset")
Reported-by: Stefan Teodorescu <fane@google.com>
Reported-by: Oren Yomtov <thenameisoren@gmail.com>
Reported-by: Chris Mason <mason@kernel.org>
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Cc: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
Signed-off-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Message-ID: <20261022015407.279870-2-seanjc@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c | 27 ++++++++++++++++-----------
 1 file changed, 16 insertions(+), 11 deletions(-)

[PATCH 4/7] KVM: nVMX: Process each host MSR entry at most once when unwinding failed VM-Entry
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Add a missing "break" in the inner for-loop of the open coded mess that
unwinds MSRs back to the pre-VM-Enter state when VMLAUNCH/VMRESUME VM-Fails
due to a consistency check that is caught by hardware but missed by KVM.
I.e. move on to the next MSR in the VM-Entry load list after performing a
write using a value found in the VM-Exit load list.  Processing *every*
matching entry in the VM-Exit load list makes the processing quadratic,
which is problematic if the load lists are full of MSRs that are expensive
to emulate.  E.g. writing APIC_BASE triggers kvm_recalculate_apic_map() and
a whole pile of unnecessary work.

Functionally, arbitrarily restoring the value of the first matching entry
is "fine", because none of this is architecturally correct: the load lists
are never supposed to have been used in the first place.  In practice, it's
likely a moot point as no well-behaved VMM will have multiple, diverging
entries in the load lists.

Fixes: bd18bffca353 ("KVM: nVMX: restore host state in nested_vmx_vmexit for VMFail")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Cc: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>
Reported-by: Stefan Teodorescu <fane@google.com>
Suggested-by: Stefan Teodorescu <fane@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Message-ID: <20261027185917.1478906-1-seanjc@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

[PATCH 5/7] KVM: x86/mmu: Cap number of parent PTEs per SP at 1000 (the arbitrary rmap limit)
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


When potentially reusing a shadow page, zap the shadow page if its number
of parent PTEs exceeds KVM's (mostly) arbitrary rmap recycling threshold.
Letting the guest create a (mostly) unbounded number of parent PTEs allows
the guest to coerce KVM into creating lists containing millions of PTEs,
effectively bounded only by the maximum number of shadow pages.

Because PTE lists are simple singly linked lists, removal is O(n).  Even
worse, if a list is constructed just so, teardown can be O(n^2), e.g. if
SPTEs are built such that zapping child SPTEs always zaps the last entry
in the list.  To cap things off, all paths that walk PTE lists do so
while holding mmu_lock, without conditionally rescheduling.

Using a doubly linked list would not address the issue, as removal would
remain O(n) since KVM still wouldn't know where in the list to remove from.
Removal could be made O(1) by storing a per-SPTE pointer, but that would
largely defeat the purpose of reusing SPs, and would penalize well-behaved
guests, especially for nested TDP where aliasing page tables is uncommon.

So, cap the maximum number of parent PTEs at the rmap limit.  In practice,
well-behaved guests will likely never run afoul of the limit.  E.g. when
running Linux as L1 with TDP, the number of parent PTEs for a single SP
tops out at under 200 (though undoubtedly obscure workloads can push it
higher).  Deliberately use a "greater than or equal" as opposed to rmap's
strictly "greater than", as the rmap code checks the threshold immediately
*after* inserting into the list, i.e. the limit is 1000 for rmaps, KVM just
very briefly blows past it.

Apply the check to both indirect and direct SPs, because while a fully
direct MMU naturally limits the number of parent PTEs to '1', direct SPs in
indirect MMUs have no such constraint.  E.g. if the guest maps a huge PTE
into many locations in its page tables, and KVM is backing the hugepage
with a direct SP, i.e. with a series of smaller pages, then KVM could end
up with a direct SP with many parents.

Note, shadow_mmu_try_split_huge_pages() always restarts the rmap walk or
bails entirely if it gets to the point where it tries to grab a SP, i.e.
there is *no* pre-existing bug that would be tripped by zapping a SP and
thus rmaps when getting a SP.

Cc: Jim Mattson <jmattson@google.com>
Cc: James Houghton <jthoughton@google.com>
Cc: Yosry Ahmed <yosry@kernel.org>
Cc: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Reported-by: Stefan Teodorescu <fane@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Message-ID: <20261027171518.1432557-1-seanjc@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

[PATCH 3/7] KVM: x86: Conditionally resched when restarting REP string emulation
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


When restarting emulation, e.g. of a REP-prefixed string instruction,
conditionally reschedule as emulating guest writes can be deceptively slow
and expensive, e.g. when shadow paging is in use and each write can trigger
write tracking.

In a perfect world, restarting emulation (or not) would be less arbitrary,
e.g. would honor not only host events (NEED_RESCHED, signals, etc.) but
also guest events (e.g. by checking kvm_vcpu_has_events()).  Unfortunately,
as pointed out by Sashiko, pio_in_emulated()'s read-ahead logic is baked
into the restart logic, as KVM relies on restarting emulation to drain the
cache and avoid duplicate reads from the I/O port.  I.e. re-entering the
guest on kvm_vcpu_has_events() or __xfer_to_guest_mode_work_pending() would
introduce architecturally-incorrect, guest-visible changes.

Fixes: 5cd21917da24 ("KVM: x86 emulator: restart string instruction without going back to a guest.")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Cc: David Woodhouse <dwmw2@infradead.org>
Reported-by: Stefan Teodorescu <fane@google.com>
Suggested-by: Stefan Teodorescu <fane@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Sean Christopherson <seanjc@google.com>
Message-ID: <20261028173638.1765329-1-seanjc@google.com>

[PATCH 2/7] KVM: x86/mmu: Conditionally resched when retrying gPTE walk on A/D update failure
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Conditionally reschedule when retrying a guest PTE walk because the CMPXCHG
to update A/D bits failed, e.g. because a different task/vCPU modified the
relevant gPTEs.  On kernels without involuntary preemption of some kind, a
misbehaving guest can "capture" a pCPU effectively indefinitely and trigger
soft lockups by continuously modifying its gPTEs such that the result of
the walk is remains valid.

Fixes: b3e4e63fd9ee ("KVM: MMU: Use cmpxchg for pte updates on walk_addr()")

[PATCH 1/7] KVM: nSVM: Check validity of vmcs01 save area copied from SMRAM on RSM
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


When doing RSM to L2, validate L1's state after copying it back into vmcb01
from SMRAM, as SMRAM is carved out of guest memory, i.e. is writable by the
guest.  Failure to validate L1's state is especially problematic for inputs
to MMU context, as KVM can end up with impossible CR4 vs. EFER state.  E.g.
without the recent hardening FNAME(walk_addr_generic), a guest can trigger:

[PATCH v3 1/3] vfio/pci: pass FAULT_FLAG_WRITE to vmf_insert_pfn_pmd/pud()
Author: Mina Almasry <almasrymina@google.com>

vfio_pci_vmf_insert_pfn() is the only caller of vmf_insert_pfn_pmd() and
vmf_insert_pfn_pud() in the kernel that hardcodes write=false instead of
passing vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE (unlike drm, panthor, and mshv).

On x86_64, normal MAP_SHARED | PROT_WRITE VFIO BAR mappings usually mask
this bug because vma->vm_page_prot already has _PAGE_RW set. However,
when vma->vm_page_prot starts read-only, insert_pmd() and insert_pud()
with write=false install a read-only huge entry without calling
pmd_mkdirty()/maybe_pmd_mkwrite() (and similarly for PUD). Subsequent
write-protection faults enter wp_huge_pmd() or wp_huge_pud(), which call
vma->vm_ops->huge_fault() to upgrade the entry in place. Because
vfio_pci_vmf_insert_pfn() passes write=false, insert_pmd() and
insert_pud() see !pmd_none(*pmd) with !write, do nothing, and return
VM_FAULT_NOPAGE, causing an unrecoverable infinite write page-fault loop
and host CPU soft lockup on the next userspace or KVM guest MMIO store.

In practice, vma->vm_page_prot is read-only on a writable MAP_SHARED BAR
mapping in two situations:

1. Userspace soft-dirty tracking (CONFIG_MEM_SOFT_DIRTY) when a process
   such as CRIU (during pre-dump/checkpoint), a VMM live-migration loop,
   a memory profiler, or an unprivileged process holding a VFIO device
   fd writes "4" (CLEAR_REFS_SOFT_DIRTY) to /proc/<pid>/clear_refs. That
   clears VM_SOFTDIRTY on all VMAs (including VM_PFNMAP) and calls
   vma_set_page_prot(), stripping _PAGE_RW from vma->vm_page_prot for
   subsequent huge faults.

2. Architectures where shared writable mappings start read-only for
   software dirty tracking, such as arm64 without hardware DBM
   (CONFIG_ARM64_HW_AFDBM off or FEAT_HAFDBS absent), where _PAGE_SHARED
   sets PTE_RDONLY | PTE_WRITE and pgprot_noncached() preserves
   PTE_RDONLY on MT_DEVICE_nGnRnE mappings until pmd_mkdirty() clears
   it on a write fault.

This bug was found by code inspection while auditing huge_fault handlers
and reproduced with a VFIO PCI selftest on a 2 MiB-aligned BAR mapping
after writing "4" to /proc/self/clear_refs.

Pass vmf->flags & FAULT_FLAG_WRITE to vmf_insert_pfn_pmd() and
vmf_insert_pfn_pud() so write faults properly mark huge PFNMAP entries
dirty and writable.

Fixes: f9e54c3a2f5b ("vfio/pci: implement huge_fault support")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Cc: Ankit Agrawal <ankita@nvidia.com>
Cc: Ritesh Harjani (IBM) <ritesh.list@gmail.com>
Cc: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Mina Almasry <almasrymina@google.com>
---
 drivers/vfio/pci/vfio_pci_core.c | 6 ++++--
 1 file changed, 4 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

[PATCH v3 3/3] vfio: selftests: Test BAR write faults after clear_refs
Author: Mina Almasry <almasrymina@google.com>

Add a regression test in vfio_pci_device_test to verify that huge_fault
on writable MAP_SHARED VFIO PCI BAR mappings handles write faults after
soft-dirty tracking clears VM_SOFTDIRTY and downgrades vma->vm_page_prot
to read-only via /proc/self/clear_refs ("4").

Use vfio_pci_find_mem_bar() to locate a memory-backed BAR of at least
2 MiB that is safe to read and write, and zap existing BAR mappings via
PCI_COMMAND_MEMORY before each sub-test to verify both:

1. Read-then-write fault sequence after clear_refs, exercising in-place
   huge PMD/PUD write-protection upgrade via wp_huge_pmd()/wp_huge_pud().
2. Direct initial write fault on an unpopulated BAR VMA after clear_refs,
   exercising vmf_insert_pfn_pmd()/vmf_insert_pfn_pud() with
   FAULT_FLAG_WRITE set.

Skip gracefully when the device has no known memory-backed BAR >= 2 MiB
or when /proc/self/clear_refs soft-dirty tracking (CONFIG_MEM_SOFT_DIRTY)
is unavailable.

Suggested-by: Alex Williamson <alex@shazbot.org>

Signed-off-by: Mina Almasry <almasrymina@google.com>

[PATCH v5 0/2] x86/tdx: Use TDVMCALLs directly for earlyprintk
Author: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>

Background
==========

TDX guests have early_printk support, but it is through a roundabout way
using a #VE exception as a functional mechanism. The exception handler
has to use TDG.VP.VEINFO.GET to find out what faulted, and then issue
the TDVMCALL. Simplify this by using a TDVMCALL from the guest which can
directly perform the port I/O.

Patch details
=============

Patch 1 is a cleanup that moves the port I/O direction constants to
arch/x86/include/asm/shared/tdx.h.

Patch 2: Add a new earlyprintk=tdx option, which calls the new
tdx_serial_in() / tdx_serial_out() helpers to perform the port I/O
directly using TDVMCALLs.

The serial earlyprintk path is left as is - TD guests using this will
still take a #VE exception, and the exception handler will perform the
I/O.

Testing
=======

Added a counter for the number of COM1 port I/O #VEs handled:

  no earlyprintk     -  ~63000
  earlyprintk=ttyS0  -  ~61000
  earlyprintk=tdx    -  ~35000

The new tdx option nearly halves the number of #VEs handled.

Additionally, ran the usual suite of CI testing, which covers KVM self
tests, KVM unit tests, and Avocado KVM tests without any regressions.

LLM Use
=======

LLMs were used under supervision to generate some of the changes, to
help understand the scope and mechanisms, create testing instrumentation
(throwaway) to count #VEs in the serial vs tdx earlyprintk setups, and
to drive lab machines to do this testing.

Signed-off-by: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>
---

[PATCH v5 2/2] x86/early_printk: Add earlyprintk=tdx to drive the
Author: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>

A TDX guest cannot execute port I/O instructions directly, but
earlyprintk's serial console still issues plain inb()/outb() and lets
each one fault into the #VE handler to be emulated as a TDVMCALL.

While that works, it is a roundabout way to get a character out.
early_serial_putc() polls the LSR, and then writes a byte, but since the
TDX guest can't directly do port I/O, a #VE exception is raised. The #VE
handler must call TDG.VP.VEINFO.GET to find out what faulted, and then
it can issue the TDVMCALL that does the actual work.

This makes #VE a functional mechanism for doing I/O, which is not
desirable, is unnecessarily complicated and fragile, and results in
twice the number of calls into the TDX module.

Instead, add an earlyprintk=tdx option, which can issue the TDVMCALLs
directly. Add a pair of tdx_serial_in() and tdx_serial_out() accessors
and set them up in this case.

Note that the output does not appear any earlier - "earlyprintk=" is an
early_param(), so the console is still registered from
parse_early_param(). This only changes how the bytes leave the guest
once it is up.

Signed-off-by: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Rick Edgecombe <rick.p.edgecombe@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Nikolay Borisov <nik.borisov@suse.com>
---
 Documentation/admin-guide/kernel-parameters.txt |  8 +++++++
 arch/x86/include/asm/tdx.h                      |  4 ++++
 arch/x86/coco/tdx/tdx.c                         | 29 +++++++++++++++++++++++++
 arch/x86/kernel/early_printk.c                  | 20 +++++++++++++++++
 4 files changed, 61 insertions(+)

[PATCH v2] x86/tdx: Remove the early #VE handler
Author: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>

tdx_early_handle_ve() emulates port I/O for #VEs taken before
idt_setup_early_traps() installs the real #VE gate. Per commit
32e72854fa5f ("x86/tdx: Port I/O: Add early boot support"), it exists to
support earlyprintk's serial driver.

"earlyprintk=" and "earlycon=" are both early_param() handlers, so no
console can be registered before parse_early_param(), which runs from
setup_arch(), after idt_setup_early_traps() has replaced the early IDT.
Nothing in the window between idt_setup_early_handler() and
idt_setup_early_traps() does port I/O, so this emulation doesn't ever
get called.

Remove the handler. Any #VE in the early window now falls through to
early_fixup_exception().

Signed-off-by: Vishal Verma <vishal.l.verma@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Rick Edgecombe <rick.p.edgecombe@intel.com>
Reviewed-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Nikolay Borisov <nik.borisov@suse.com>
Cc: Andi Kleen <ak@linux.intel.com>
Cc: Rick Edgecombe <rick.p.edgecombe@intel.com>
---
Changes in v2:
- Add review tags (Rick, Kiryl, Nikolay)
- Move LLM attribution below the ---
- Rebase to v7.4-rc1
- Link to v1: https://patch.msgid.link/20260910-b4-tdx_remove_early_ve_handler-v1-1-bf47a4d360c5@intel.com
---
Found with the help of an LLM observing that the real IDT is installed
before parse_early_param(). LLMs were also used to verify this. On a TDX
guest booted with earlyprintk, a counter added to tdx_early_handle_ve()
for testing remained 0 after boot.

[PATCH rdma-next v2 10/15] vfio/mlx5: Enable relaxed ordering on the live migration data mkey
Author: Michael Gur <michaelgur@nvidia.com>

As firmware guarantees that all device-issued data writes complete before
posting of CQE, there is no need for strong ordering of memory accesses
on the live migration data mkey.
Enable relaxed ordering on the mkey via the mlx5_core RO setter.

This allows better performance and supporting vfio live migration on HW
that restricts memory registration with strict ordering.

[PATCH kvmtool v4 00/23] arm64: Support GICv5-based guests
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

This series adds support for GICv5-based VMs to kvmtool. These are
exposed as --irqchip=gicv5 and --irqchip=gicv5-its, where the former
only creates a vGICv5 device and the latter adds in a GICv5 ITS. The
GICv5 specification can be found at [1].

The GICv5 support in KVM needed by --irqchip=gicv5 can be found as of
Linux v7.4-rc1. KVM support for GICv5 ITS has been posted at
[2]. Thus, --irqchip=gicv5 works with the corresponding KVM changes,
while --irqchip=gicv5-its cannot yet be used with upstream KVM.

The series teaches kvmtool to create the GICv5 irqchip and ITS KVM
devices. It describes the GICv5 CPU interface, IRS and ITS in the
guest DT, updates the timer and PMU interrupt descriptions, and
generates GICv5 interrupt specifiers for platform devices and PCI. It
also maps PCI MSI requester IDs to the ITS.

Since [3], there have been a lot of changes to the core plumbing
introduced by the GICv5 support. Thanks to Vladimir's suggestions, it
has been somewhat decoupled from the GICv5 support itself, and the
split is now much cleaner. Thanks, Vladimir! The IRS support is built
up and exposed before the ITS support is added, as was the case
previously. This allows, for example, the --irqchip=gicv5 config to be
merged while leaving the ITS changes under review.

Compared with v3 [3]:

* Split GIC device address setup into CPU interface, redistributor and
  distributor helpers. The existing MSI helper now uses the global MSI
  base, and the GICv2 PPI CPU-mask check uses irqchip ordering.

* Split IRQ-offset support into generic infrastructure and separate
  GICv5 configuration.

* Use configured SPI/PPI bases and FDT types in interrupt and DT code,
  with a helper for encoding the PMU's GICv5 PPI INTID. Remove the
  unused GICv5 state.

* Give GICv5 FDT interrupt types distinct names and emit CPU phandles
  for all arm64 guests; the GICv5 IRS references those phandles.

* Sync the UAPI headers with v7.4-rc1.

The v7.4-rc1 header sync includes GICv5 IRS UAPI, but not GICv5 ITS
UAPI. Patch 19 mirrors the ITS definitions from the pending KVM series
[2]. Once that UAPI lands upstream, the headers should be resynced
against the latest Linux headers before merging the ITS support.

Thanks,
Sascha

[1] https://developer.arm.com/documentation/aes0070/latest
[2] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261102194454.4086911-1-sascha.bischoff@arm.com/
[3] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261016153939.913764-1-sascha.bischoff@arm.com/

Sascha Bischoff (23):

  arm64: Close the ITS descriptor when setup fails
  arm64: Split GIC device setup
  Sync kernel UAPI headers with v7.4-rc1

[PATCH v2] vfio: selftests: Remove libuuid dependency
Author: Samiullah Khawaja <skhawaja@google.com>

Libvfio depends on an external dependency libuuid for parsing vf_tokens.
Building it in cross compilation environments gives the following error,

 ld: cannot find -luuid: No such file or directory

It also complicates building with glibc alternatives like musl, see
https://lore.kernel.org/kvm/c2f7e904-2646-4b01-864d-d185221c7f56@gmail.com/

As it is only used for parsing vf_tokens, remove the dependency by
adding a minimal uuid_t and uuid_parse() to libvfio.

Tested by running vfio_pci_sriov_uapi_test selftest.

Signed-off-by: Samiullah Khawaja <skhawaja@google.com>
---

- Move the parser to lib/uuid.c and lib/include/libvfio/uuid.h (David)
- Drop the vfio_ prefixes and add a uuid_t (David)
- Mention musl in the commit message (David)

[PATCH v2 15/16] KVM: selftests: Add VGICv5 ITS translation cache tests
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Add end-to-end coverage for VGICv5 ITS translation and cache
invalidation. The ITS attribute test only checks the userspace API
surface; this test verifies that KVM can parse guest-provided linear
DT and ITT state and use it to deliver LPIs through guest GEN_EVENTR
and KVM_SIGNAL_MSI.

Build a two-VPE VM with IRS, ITS and an LPI IST. VPE0 programs a
linear device table and interrupt translation table, enables the
virtual ITS, enables two LPIs targeting VPE1, and sends GEN_EVENTR to
populate the translation cache with EventID to LPI A. VPE1 reports the
LPI identity it consumed.

VPE0 then rewrites the ITT to point at LPI B without invalidation, and
a host KVM_SIGNAL_MSI injection verifies that the stale cached LPI A
translation is reused. VPE0 invalidates the event with ITS_INV_EVENTR
and the host verifies that LPI B is delivered.

VPE0 then rewrites the ITT back to LPI A, invalidates the device with
ITS_INV_DEVICER, and the host verifies that LPI A is delivered again.
This exercises the guest-side event and device invalidation paths
rather than invalidating translations directly from the test host.

Also cover negative MSI paths by checking that KVM_SIGNAL_MSI rejects a
message without KVM_MSI_VALID_DEVID and rejects a message whose address
does not target the GICv5 ITS. Finally, inject once before guest-side
ITS disable to prove delivery still works, then have VPE0 disable the
ITS and verify that a cached KVM_SIGNAL_MSI delivery is rejected with
ENXIO.

Check 32-bit accesses to both halves of DT_BASER and preservation of
the untouched half. Verify that guest writes to the ITS ID registers
and READ_EVENT_DATAR are ignored, and that READ_EVENT_DATAR can be read
at both 32-bit and 64-bit widths.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 tools/testing/selftests/kvm/arm64/vgic_v5.c   | 518 +++++++++++++++++-
 .../selftests/kvm/include/arm64/gic_v5.h      |  17 +
 2 files changed, 532 insertions(+), 3 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 14/16] KVM: selftests: Add VGICv5 ITS attribute tests
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Add coverage for the VGICv5 ITS device address and ITS_REGS attribute
groups. Userspace needs to be able to discover and save the virtual
ITS register state, while KVM must reject invalid address placement
and malformed register accesses.

Verify that the ITS device exposes only the ITS address type, reports
VGIC_ADDR_UNDEF before configuration, rejects misaligned and
out-of-IPA bases, accepts a valid 64K-aligned base, reports it back,
and rejects a second base assignment.

Exercise ITS_REGS after the ITS base is configured by checking all
exposed offsets, read/write-back access, bad offsets, bad alignment,
and bad userspace pointers. Also initialise the shared VM wrapper so
non-ITS tests safely ignore the optional ITS fd during cleanup.

Verify that nonzero IDR0-IDR2, READ_EVENT_DATAR and DT_BASER values
round-trip through ITS_REGS, and reject unsupported ID register
values.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 13/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Implement irq forwarding
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

GICv5, like GICv4, supports direct injection of interrupts via the
ITS. Move the existing GICv4 interface behind a common shim that selects
the appropriate implementation. For GICv5 forwarding, the ITS driver
replaces a host ITTE with one targeting a guest. The host no longer
receives that interrupt until forwarding is removed and the host ITTE is
restored.

A guest can make multiple DeviceID/EventID pairs resolve to the same
LPI, even though it should not. Track the cache entry responsible for
direct injection, and let only that entry tear it down during guest
invalidation. Explicitly unforwarding an IRQ clears the owner without
removing the cached translation.

The ITS driver writes the virtual ITTE before invalidating its cache.
If invalidation times out, attempt to restore the host ITTE before
falling back to software injection. If the restoration also times out,
keep the host IRQ association and a separate LPI reference so
unforwarding can be retried even after the guest invalidates the
translation. Clear KVM's forwarding state only after the host ITTE has
been restored.

Forwarding can be set up from another process's address space through
IRQ bypass. A cache miss in this path must still walk the guest's DT and
ITT. Read the entries from the VM's mm using access_remote_vm() when
the caller does not have that mm. Hold an mm reference and protect the
memslot lookup with SRCU, and fail the lookup on a short read.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/arm.c              |   6 +-
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-its-v5.c | 129 +++++++++++++++++++++-----
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-mmio.c   |  10 +-

[PATCH v2 11/16] irqchip/gic-v5: Enable direct injection in ITS
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Allow KVM to replace a host ITTE with a virtual translation that
directly injects an interrupt into a guest. Add an irq_chip callback to
install and remove the virtual translation, and disable lazy masking
while the interrupt is forwarded. The KVM caller is added later.

The ITS writes the ITTE before invalidating its cache. If invalidation
times out, hardware may still use the virtual translation even though
setup failed. Allow disable to restore the host ITTE when the forwarded
flag is clear, and retain retryable state if restoration also times
out. Keep lazy masking disabled until the host mapping is restored.

Co-authored-by: Timothy Hayes <timothy.hayes@arm.com>
Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 10/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS userspace register access
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Userspace needs access to the emulated GICv5 ITS MMIO state in order
to inspect and restore the device state around VM reset and migration.
Expose the GICv5 ITS register frame through KVM_DEV_ARM_VGIC_GRP_ITS_REGS.

Reuse the same MMIO register descriptors used by the guest-facing
IODEV, but apply the usual userspace access constraints: the ITS base
address must have been configured, the register offset must map to an
emulated ITS register, and userspace accesses must use the natural
32-bit or 64-bit register alignment.

Add control handling for KVM_DEV_ARM_ITS_CTRL_RESET. The reset is
performed while all vCPUs are stopped and while holding the ITS lock,
so it cannot race with guest MMIO updates or translation lookups. The
reset clears guest-programmed state and cached translations while
preserving the advertised ITS capability defaults.

Do not expose GICv5 ITS SAVE_TABLES or RESTORE_TABLES controls. For
GICv5, the guest-owned DT and ITTs are the authoritative translation
state, and the KVM translation cache is disposable state that can be
rebuilt from guest memory.

Restore guest read-only IDR0-IDR2 through dedicated userspace writes,
validating the advertised capabilities before changing them. Also
allow userspace to restore READ_EVENT_DATAR while keeping guest writes
to that result register ignored.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-its-v5.c | 371 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++----
 1 file changed, 322 insertions(+), 49 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 05/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS device skeleton
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Introduce the basic KVM device plumbing for a GICv5 ITS. This adds the
GICv5 ITS device type and address attribute, registers the device ops,
and wires a GICv5 ITS IODEV into the generic VGIC MMIO dispatch path.

Add struct vgic_v5_its to hold the ITS base address, the IODEV plumbing,
the backing KVM device pointer, and the general ITS mutex. Allocate this
state from the KVM device create path, initialise the mutex and base
address, and mark the VM as having an ITS. GICv5 MSIs require a DeviceID,
so set msis_require_devid when the ITS is created.

Record the VGIC model required by an ITS and reject mismatched device
creation in either order. Check GICv3 ITS creation as well, so the two
ITS models cannot be combined in one VM.

The MMIO region is registered once userspace provides the ITS base
address through KVM_VGIC_V5_ADDR_TYPE_ITS. For now the IODEV only
provides a RAZ/WI placeholder region; the actual ITS register emulation
is added separately.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 09/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS MSI injection
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Now that KVM can resolve GICv5 ITS translations from guest memory,
allow userspace and irqfd MSI injection to target a virtual GICv5 ITS.

Add helpers to validate that an MSI address resolves to a GICv5 ITS
IODEV, find the ITS for an MSI, resolve the DeviceID/EventID pair to a
vgic_irq, and inject the resulting LPI with the GICv5 VDPEND path.
GICv5 MSIs require KVM_MSI_VALID_DEVID, as the DeviceID is part of the
ITS translation key.

Although MSI data is 32 bits, GICv5 supports only 16-bit EventIDs.
Reject a nonzero upper half before narrowing the EventID for cache
lookups, so malformed MSI data cannot alias a valid translation.

Provide both the slow path, which resolves the translation from guest
memory and populates the ITS cache, and the inatomic cached path used
by irqfd. If the cached path misses, irqfd retries in a work item. The
worker borrows the VM's mm while walking the guest tables, then drops
it before injecting the LPI.

Wire kvm_set_msi() and kvm_arch_set_irq_inatomic() to use the GICv5
ITS injection helpers for GICv5 VMs, while keeping the existing GICv3
ITS path unchanged for GICv3 VMs.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-irqfd.c  |   8 ++
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-its-v5.c | 132 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++--
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic.h        |   6 ++

[PATCH v2 08/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS translation cache maintenance
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Walking the guest DT and ITTs on every MSI or ITS event is unnecessary
once a DeviceID/EventID pair has been resolved. Add an XArray indexed
by the DeviceID/EventID pair to cache resolved translations as a
disposable acceleration structure, avoiding repeated walks of guest
memory.

Cache entries hold references to the resolved struct vgic_irq objects
stored in the VM-wide LPI xarray. Lookups use RCU to access entries in
interrupt context without taking a raw spinlock, and take an IRQ
reference before returning an entry to the caller. Cache eviction drops
the reference held by the cache and defers freeing the entry until RCU
readers have finished. This keeps the cache tied to the normal LPI
object lifetime instead of storing raw LPI numbers.

Invalidate cached translations when the table configuration or guest
invalidation commands make them stale. Changes to DT_BASER or DT_CFGR
drop the entire cache, while writes of unchanged values leave it intact.
INV_EVENTR removes either one event or the events covered by an L1 ITT
entry, and INV_DEVICER removes the translations covered by either an L2
DT entry or an L1 DT entry.

The cache is not part of any userspace ABI and is never authoritative:
entries can always be rebuilt from guest memory on the next lookup.

The vgic_irq objects for LPIs track valid translations rather than
mirror interrupt state. Mirroring that state would require querying
the GIC on every guest exit. Once direct injection is added, the
objects also record whether an LPI is mapped into the host ITT.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-its-v5.c | 219 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++--
 include/kvm/arm_vgic.h            |   3 +
 2 files changed, 208 insertions(+), 14 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 07/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS guest table translation emulation
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

The GICv5 ITS consumes guest-owned translation tables. Unlike the
GICv3 ITS, KVM does not maintain a separate software device/table
model for GICv5 translations; the guest's DT and ITTs are the
authoritative source of DeviceID/EventID to LPI mappings.

Add helpers to walk both linear and two-level guest Device Tables and
Interrupt Translation Tables. The walker reads the guest table entries
under the ITS lock, validates the relevant valid bits and range fields,
and resolves a DeviceID/EventID pair to the LPI encoded in the guest's
ITTE.

Use the table walker to implement the translation side effects of the
MMIO interface. READ_EVENTR now resolves DIDR/EIDR and reports the LPI
through READ_EVENT_DATAR, while GEN_EVENTR resolves GEN_EVENT_DIDR and
GEN_EVENT_EIDR and injects the resulting LPI into the VM.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---

 1 file changed, 300 insertions(+)

[PATCH v2 06/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS MMIO state emulation
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

In order to expose a virtual GICv5 ITS to the guest, KVM needs to
emulate the ITS CONFIG_FRAME and its translation-control registers.
Replace the placeholder ITS IODEV region with the GICv5 ITS register
layout and handlers for the architected MMIO state.

Provide read/write emulation for the ITS ID registers, IIDR, AIDR,
CR0, CR1, DT_BASER, DT_CFGR, DIDR, EIDR, invalidation registers,
READ_EVENT, GEN_EVENT, status registers, and the RAZ/WI MMIO
registers that KVM does not implement. Reuse the shared CoreSight
identification-register helper so the ITS reports the same component
identification values as the IRS.

At this stage the translation-triggering registers only record state.
Walking the guest's DT/ITT, generating READ_EVENT results, and
injecting GEN_EVENT LPIs are added by later commits.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 01/16] KVM: arm64: vgic: Split out common LPI allocation
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

The GICv3 ITS code currently owns vgic_add_lpi(), even though the
first half of the helper is not ITS specific. It allocates or finds
the VM-wide vgic_irq object in lpi_xa and initialises the common LPI
state before the GICv3 ITS code reads the property table and pending
state.

GICv5 needs to reuse the same LPI object lifetime and refcounting, but
without reading the GICv3 property table. Move the allocation and
xarray insertion path into vgic_alloc_lpi() in vgic.c. Both
vgic_add_lpi() and the GICv3 ITS wrapper perform an initial lookup,
then call this helper on a miss. The ITS wrapper continues to load the
property and pending state, while GICv5 can use the common allocator
without GICv3 setup.

Keeping the lookup outside vgic_alloc_lpi() preserves GICv3 behaviour
when another caller inserts the LPI during allocation: the ITS wrapper
still performs its setup on the returned LPI. A second lookup inside
the allocator could instead treat it as already initialised and skip
that setup.

Initialise LPIs as Group 1 before publishing them in lpi_xa. GICv3
LPIs are Group 1, and another vCPU can find a new LPI as soon as it is
published. GICv5 does not use the group field.

This preserves the intended GICv3 behaviour while making the common
LPI storage usable by other VGIC users.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-its.c | 75 ++++---------------------------
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic.c     | 82 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic.h     |  4 ++
 3 files changed, 95 insertions(+), 66 deletions(-)

The ITS MMIO callbacks currently take a struct vgic_its pointer,

[PATCH v2 00/16] KVM: arm64: Add GICv5 ITS support
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Hi all,

This is the second posting of my GICv5 KVM ITS series.

This series builds on the GICv5 IRS support now in 7.4-rc1 and adds
support for the GICv5 ITS, as described by the GICv5 specification
[1]. It allows a GICv5 guest to receive LPIs generated by MSIs,
including those from physical PCI devices mapped into the guest via
VFIO.

The ITS is exposed as a separate KVM device, alongside the VGICv5
device. The guest owns the device table and interrupt translation
tables. KVM walks these tables to resolve a DeviceID and EventID to an
LPI, and caches the result until the guest invalidates the relevant
translation or changes the table configuration.

The main pieces are:

* An emulated GICv5 ITS CONFIG_FRAME, including table configuration,
  event generation, and invalidation registers.

* Guest table walking for linear and two-level device and interrupt
  translation tables, together with a disposable XArray-based
  translation cache.

* MSI injection through KVM_SIGNAL_MSI and irqfd. GICv5 requires a
  DeviceID for each MSI, and the virtual ITS supports 16-bit EventIDs.

* Direct injection through the host ITS when an interrupt is forwarded
  to a vCPU. Forwarding teardown restores the host translation.

* Userspace attributes to configure the ITS address, access its
  registers, and reset the emulated state, together with ABI
  documentation.

* Selftests for the ITS attributes, guest table translation, MSI
  delivery, and guest-driven cache invalidation.

The guest tables remain the authoritative translation state. KVM's
cache can be discarded and rebuilt from guest memory; there is no
separate ITS table data for the VMM to save/restore.

Lorenzo's GICv5 Linux software enablement guide [2] describes how to
set up the GICv5 software stack. These changes have been tested on an
Arm FVP 11.32 release [3].

These changes are based on v7.4-rc1. The corresponding kvmtool patches
have been posted separately [4]. I plan to post an updated kvmtool
series shortly.

These changes are also available as a branch at [5]. For reference, v1
of this series can be found at [6].

This series applies independently of the host GICv5 fixes [7], but
concurrent use may encounter the host driver issues described there. A
branch with both series is available at [8]. Both series need to be
merged for the ITS support to be complete.

The main changes since v1 are:

* Rebased onto v7.4-rc1.

* Reused upstream ITS model tracking, removing the separate has_its
  state.

* Sashiko flagged some races for GICv3 LPIs, which were caused by me
  making the allocation common to both vITSes. Group 1 is set before
  adding the LPI to the tracking XArray. The GICv3 ITS path performs
  one initial lookup before allocating a new LPI.

* Replaced the translation cache hash table with an XArray. This
  avoids the locking/lockup issues raised by Sashiko. Furthermore, it
  is no longer possible for a malicious guest to force all LPI
  mappings into one hashtable bucket.

* Tightened translation and MSI handling: enforce advertised DeviceID
  and EventID limits, report invalid guest translations as blocked
  MSIs, and allow software injection to proceed if caching fails.

* Expanded ITS register handling and the userspace ABI: serialise
  register access, support 32-bit accesses to 64-bit registers, and
  validate restoration of IDR0-IDR2 and READ_EVENT_DATAR while keeping
  them read-only to the guest.

* Hardened direct injection teardown: track which translation owns
  forwarding, synchronise host translation restoration, and retain the
  IRQ association when a timeout requires a later retry.

* Extended selftests for register access widths, read-only behaviour,
  ID limits, malformed MSIs, and cache invalidation.

Thanks for taking the time to look at these changes!

Thanks,
Sascha

[1] https://support.arm.com/documentation/111701/latest
[2] https://linaro.atlassian.net/wiki/x/CQAF-wY
[3] https://support.arm.com/tools-and-software/fixed-virtual-platforms/arm-architecture-fvps
[4] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261016153939.913764-1-sascha.bischoff@arm.com

[PATCH 44/45] KVM: x86: selftests: Introduce memory attributes side-channel tests
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Introduce memory attributes selftests to catch any vulnerable
side-channels.

Memory attributes access restrictions are vulnerable to side-channel
attacks. This means that any KVM operation initiated by the guest that
requires guest memory access (which is the case for most
para-virtualised interfaces) needs to consider memory attributes.

The tests confirm this requirement is upheld for a variety of
use-cases, exercising various kinds of guest memory access such as
kvm_read/write_guest(), gfn_to_hva_cache and gfn_to_pfn_cache.

Co-developed-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>
Signed-off-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-32-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

[PATCH 43/45] KVM: x86: selftests: Introduce memory attributes PTE test
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Add more memory attributes tests to check that memory protection
restricts accesses even when installed on a page that holds guest page
table entries. Notably two cases are taken into account:
- The page is made non-accesible. In such case the next access to a
  virtual memory address translated by that paging structure should
  fault.
- The page is made read-only. In such case the next access to a virtual
  memory address translated by that paging structure should either fault,
  or succeed yet not perform any writes into the PTE (accessed and
  dirty bits).

Co-developed-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>
Signed-off-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>

[PATCH 25/45] KVM: Take memory protections into account for accesses with cached gfn->hva
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Account for memory attributes when accessing guest memory through
kvm_get/put_guest().

This requires tracking the memory attributes generation as part of
gfn_to_hva_cache's data, invalidate the cached information if the
generation changes, and failing to refresh the cache if restrictive
memory attributes are found within the GPA range.

Similar to how gfn_to_hva_cache disallows caching gfns mapped within
read-only memory slots, gfns marked as read-only by memory attributes
will also fail to initialize. Unsurprisingly, the same behaviour applies
to gfns mapped as non-accessible (NR/NW), while non-executable mappings
are okay.

[PATCH 37/45] KVM: x86/mmu: Issue memory fault exit if walk failed due to memory attribute
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

During execution, page table entries are subject to memory protection
simply because their GPA is obtained with an EPT walk.  During emulation,
the checks need to be done by hand and result in an -EFAULT userspace
exit.

However, if a guest PTE is read-only, do not go all the way out to
userspace and just stomp out the write.  This is consistent with the way
KVM handles read-only memory slots during emulation, though admittedly
inconsistent with everything else: NPT for example always wants nested
page table entries for the guest page tables to be writable, and will
fault.

[PATCH 39/45] KVM: VMX: mark VM dead on shadow VMCS or eVMCS access failures
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

KVM ignores failures when reading or writing L1's in-memory VMCSs (shadow
VMCS, eVMCS), allowing execution to continue with stale shadow VMCS values
or silently losing updates.  Check both the HVA cache initialization
and the cached memory accesses, and mark the VM dead on failure; these
failures can only occur when guest memory permissions deny the access,
and terminating the VM is safe (though it precludes using memory attributes
as a replacement for e.g. userfaultfd, and only leaves the feature usable
for security purposes).

Likewise, treat a failed read of an enabled VP assist page as fatal too,
otherwise KVM continues by disabling enlightened VMCS.

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/hyperv.c |  7 ++++++-
 arch/x86/kvm/vmx/nested.c | 21 ++++++++++++++-------
 2 files changed, 20 insertions(+), 8 deletions(-)

[PATCH 20/45] KVM: Take memory protections into account for memory read/write/fetch
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Take into account memory attributes when accessing guest memory through
the kvm_{read,write,fetch}*() family of functions.

All of these pass a struct kvm_memory_slot pointer to the actual
workhorse functions, in order to share code between the VM-wide and

[PATCH 30/45] KVM: x86/emulate: Propagate memory faults to userspace
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Full handling of memory faults is not really possible with KVM's
architecture, short of adding something like a journal to complex
operations like task switches or SMM enter/leave.  However, KVM
also should at least try to propagate faults to userspace, as an
emulation failure if it is not possible to return them as -EFAULT.

Introduce new return values for both the emulator ops
(X86EMUL_MEMORY_FAULT) and emulate.c (EMULATION_MEMORY_FAULT), and
plumb them through the entire call chain.  Terminate them with
-EFAULT as a best effort.

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

returns before resetting the MMU context and with stale PDPTRs.  Mark the
VM dead on the error path, so that the vCPU cannot run with inconsistent

[PATCH 33/45] KVM: x86: Allow page fault fixup to return errors
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

kvm_fixup_and_inject_pf_error() reflects intercepted hardware page
faults into the guest, walking the guest page tables when necessary
to obtain an error code consistent with the guest's paging rules.
For example, a GPA that exceeds guest.MAXPHYADDR but fits within
host.MAXPHYADDR must cause a reserved-bit fault, even though hardware
does not report one.

Allow the function's callers to propagate memory attribute failures
from the page table walk; subsequent changes will return from
kvm_fixup_and_inject_pf_error() any error from the page-table walk.

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

[PATCH 24/45] KVM: Introduce a generation number for memory attributes
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Introduce a generation number to track memory attribute modifications.
This will allow KVM components to invalidate any assumptions they might
have about guest's physical addresses and their permissions when memory
attributes change.

Like with memory slot updates, it's mandatory for components that access
guest memory based on cached information to do so within a KVM SRCU
read-side critical section, and that they validate the generation number
before accessing memory. This, in combination with the
synchronize_srcu() call within the memory attributes ioctl handler,
ensures the following:
- A memory attribute modification operation only returns after all
  users of outdated GPA data are done running.
- Any component accessing cached data after the memory attribute
  modification returned will see the updated generation number.

Additionally, loads/stores of the generation number have
acquire/release semantics; which ensures all attribute writes are
visible before updating the generation, and loads from attributes happen
after having read the current generation number.

Ultimately, since synchronize_srcu_expedited() is an expensive
operation, only perform it when absolutely necessary. Do so if the
introduced memory attribute is known to require synchronization or if
the attribute being cleared contained a memory attribute that required
synchronization.

There shouldn't be any performance loss for memory attributes that
don't require synchronization (and in general for any VMM that does not
apply memory protections), because the attributes generation will always
remain unchanged.

Signed-off-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-22-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/loongarch/kvm/vcpu.c  |  6 ++-
 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c         |  4 +-
 include/linux/kvm_host.h   | 33 +++++++++++++--
 include/linux/kvm_types.h  |  6 ++-
 include/trace/events/kvm.h | 14 +++++--

[PATCH 28/45] KVM: x86/mmu: Take memory protection attributes into account during faults
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Take memory protection attributes when faulting guest memory.  Prohibited
memory accesses will cause a user-space -EFAULT exit just like private
memory accesses.  Userspace will either enable the access or bump it to
the guest as some kind of exception (e.g. a VTL return).

Since the struct kvm_page_fault already has the access type in PFERR_*
format, the check is done via the kvm_page_format permissions table.
This means that it supports naturally all page table format variants,
and it can even handle mode-based memory protection when the host
uses MBEC/GMET.  The only thing that needs some care is to build the
restricted ACC_* mask with the root page's own access mask as a base
(and not ACC_ALL).  Otherwise, supervisor mode execution would be
handled incorrectly on AMD processors with GMET.

To avoid spamming the trace buffer too much, the new trace event only
kicks in if memory protection attributes are present for the faulted gfn.

Signed-off-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-26-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c          | 49 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu_internal.h | 19 +++++++++++++

[PATCH 21/45] KVM: Take memory protections into account for __kvm_vcpu_map

[PATCH 17/45] KVM: Introduce NR/NW/NX memory attributes
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


Introduce memory attributes to map guest physical memory regions as
non-readable, non-writable, and/or non-executable. Only a subset of flag
combinations is supported. Notably write and exec permissions require
read permission, and memory protection attributes are incompatible with
private memory.

As mentioned in 5a475554db1e ("KVM: Introduce per-page memory attributes",
2023-11-13), bits 0-2 of the memory attributes were reserved for RWX
protection; they are negated to support current memory attribute users
which use 0 to indicate no special treatment.  Since 0 is not available,
a non-negated version of the flags would need an extra bit to express
no-access (R=0/W=0/X=0) mappings.

Unfortunately this precaution did not age too well; KVM now supports
MBEC/GMET and adding mode-based memory protections will require a
non-contiguous bit.  But that's something left for later.

Different architectures may have different limitations on the set of
valid protections, for example execution-only and XU=0 mappings are
supported by Intel but not AMD processors[1].  So, add an architecture-specific
callback and add a basic implementation for x86.

[1] When adding support for MBEC/GMET, since NX would remain to mean no
    execution at all, it is possible to use either an NXS bit or two
    separate NXS/NXU bits in addition to NX.  The former would only
    support permissions that are available with either MBEC or GMET.

Signed-off-by: Nicolas Saenz Julienne <nsaenz@amazon.com>
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-14-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 Documentation/virt/kvm/api.rst  | 29 +++++++++++++--
 arch/x86/include/asm/kvm_host.h |  1 +
 arch/x86/kvm/Kconfig            |  2 +-
 arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c          | 63 ++++++++++++++++++++++++---------
 arch/x86/kvm/x86.c              |  2 --
 include/linux/kvm_host.h        | 28 +++++++++++++++

[PATCH 18/45] KVM: Include memory protections in result of gfn->hva conversion
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>


All paths that lead to guest memory accesses now need to check whether
memory attributes allow that access.  Users of gfn_to_hva,
kvm_vcpu_gfn_to_hva and their *_prot variant can get it more or
less for free via erroneous return values, so do this first.

Note however that this is not true of the variants that take a cached
kvm_memslots pointer.  These include caches (gfn-to-hva and gfn-to-pfn)
and page faults, both of which will need specific changes; but the more
optimized functions in virt/kvm/kvm_main.c such as kvm_read_guest()
and kvm_write_guest() also retrieve the memslot high in the call chain,
and therefore they will need changes in __kvm_read/write_guest_page().

[PATCH 04/45] KVM: x86/emulate: Check TSS busy-bit writes during task switches
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Task switching ignores failures when clearing the old TSS descriptor's
busy bit and setting the new descriptor's busy bit.  A memory attribute
restriction can therefore drop either write while the task switch
continues with inconsistent descriptor state.

Check both writes and stop the task switch on failure.  Memory attribute
faults reach the existing emulation-failure exit in kvm_task_switch().

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/emulate.c | 8 ++++++--
 1 file changed, 6 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH 12/45] KVM: x86/mmu: intersect writability from __kvm_faultin_pfn with fault->map_writable
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

fault->map_writable is currently a pure output of __kvm_faultin_pfn(),
which is the only thing that restricts it.  This will no longer hold once
memory protections derived from memory attributes are applied: those
compute their own access permissions that combine with those from
__kvm_faultin_pfn().

Applying them *before* faulting in the pfn lets a fault that violates
the attributes exit to userspace without the cost of gup and/or an
async #PF; but it means that permissions will then be restricted in two
independent steps, first by memory attributes and then by __kvm_faultin_pfn().
Switch fault->map_writable to that model by letting kvm_mmu_faultin_pfn()
only clear bits rather than assign them.

No functional change intended: nothing writes fault->map_writable between
the initializer and __kvm_mmu_faultin_pfn() yet, so the AND is equivalent
to the assignment it replaces.

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-9-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/mmu/mmu.c | 12 ++++++++----
 1 file changed, 8 insertions(+), 4 deletions(-)

----------------------------------------------------------------------

[PATCH 02/45] KVM: x86/hyperv: do not overwrite hc->ingpa for SIGNAL_EVENT hypercall
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

When a guest makes a HVCALL_SIGNAL_EVENT hypercall that is not fast
(i.e. has arguments in memory), kvm_hvcall_signal_event() reads the
connection ID and overwrites hc->ingpa.

If the connection ID that is valid in userspace but not registered in the
kernel conn_to_evt, KVM exits to userspace; however, hc->param still
signals that the hypercall was not fast, and userspace will then treat
the connection ID as an address.

Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Fixes: bd38b32053eb ("KVM: hyper-v: Collect hypercall params into struct")
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-2-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---
 arch/x86/kvm/hyperv.c | 15 +++++++++------
 1 file changed, 9 insertions(+), 6 deletions(-)

[PATCH 13/45] KVM: x86/mmu: Extend map_writable to a full ACC_* mask
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

Support for memory protection attributes opens the door to installing
non-executable mappings. Instead of introducing yet another member in
struct kvm_page_fault and another argument to make_spte(), make the
existing member map_writable a mask of ACC_* bits.  This also avoids the
need for make_spte() to map a single bool to either the NX bit or
the XS/XU bits together.

Unlike for mappings that are not writable because the fault did
not request write premission, it is not not necessary to track
executability for these SPTEs; the gfn is always available and it will
be possible to access the attributes directly in FNAME(sync_spte).

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
Link: https://patch.msgid.link/20260918135030.171564-10-pbonzini@redhat.com
Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>
---

[PATCH 01/45] KVM: nVMX/nSVM: check unconditionally whether CR3 is valid
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

nested_vmcb_check_save() checks CR3 legality only if long mode is active,
but it really needs to be checked in all cases - the check uses must be
zero bits of the host, and indeed nested_svm_load_cr3() later will
reject illegal CR3 in every mode.  Move the check earlier, and thus out
of nested_svm_load_cr3().

Likewise for nested_vmx_load_cr3() and nested_vmx_check_guest_state().

Signed-off-by: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

[PATCH v4 00/45] KVM: x86: Introduce memory protection attributes
Author: Paolo Bonzini <pbonzini@redhat.com>

This series introduces a mechanism to let userspace block read,
write or execute access to individual GFNs via KVM's memory
attribute mechanism, and have them reported via KVM_EXIT_MEMORY_FAULT.

KVM needs to check the attributes anytime KVM takes GPAs as input for any
action initiated by the guest; if the memory attributes are incompatible
with such action, it should be stopped.  This means that there are quite
a lot of cases to handle, and while in most cases they occur before
committing to the execution of an instructions (for example, page walks),
there are other cases where a full implementation of attributes would
basically require an implementation of transactional memory accesses:
notably (from inevitable to where I finally drew the line) nested
virtualization, emulation, SMM switch, task switch.  In the worst case,
the processor might have executed the instruction before issuing a
trap-like vmexit - and if the instruction length is not provided and
the instruction is not accessible, there's no practical alternative
to returning -EIO and marking the VM as dead.

For this reason, documentation now clarifies that invalid accesses may not be
recoverable and may be reported in the same way as MMIO.

Along the way, the patches fix some issues in the memory attributes code,
that surfaced due to having more than one attribute.  Still, no matter
how careful I tried to be, I'm ready for the absolute massacre that
Sashiko will make of this stuff.

Paolo

v3->v4:
- New patch "KVM: nVMX/nSVM: check unconditionally whether CR3 is valid"
- New patch "KVM: x86/emulate: Check TSS busy-bit writes during task switches"
- Rebase on top of new VM memory attributes code
- Use kvm_check_gen in Longarch
- New patches to surface memory faults from the emulator and from nested
  GPA translation (15, 29-36, 38-39)
- Clarify in the docs that memory faults in some cases may not be recoverable

Author: Shmuel Fomberg <shmuelfomberg@gmail.com>

hv-memory-hint (hypercall HvExtCallMemoryHeatHint) allow a guest to
notify host which memory pages will be needed in the near future.
This is not a replacement for the balloon functionality, but can
help the kernel make better decisions when memory is scare.

Author: Vipin Sharma <vipinsh@google.com>

Extend vfio_pci_liveupdate_kexec_test to exercise continuous DMA across
a Live Update when the device is in noiommu mode.

Allocate a shmem memfd backed by three 2MB THP folios (for the driver
region, source buffer, and destination buffer), map each folio at
IOVA == PA via iommu_map_identity(), and preserve the memfd in the same
Live Update session before the device so that LUO's LIFO finish order
keeps the memory alive until after the device is finished.

Before kexec, initialize the selftest driver, run a sanity memcpy, kick
off a long-running DMA memcpy, and disable MSI-X so freeze() succeeds.
After kexec, take over the device via __vfio_pci_device_init(), retrieve
and map the preserved memfd verifying that all three 2MB folios stayed
at the exact same physical addresses, attach the driver without resetting
the device, wait for the pre-kexec memcpy to complete, and run another
memcpy without reinitializing the device.

Co-developed-by: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>
Signed-off-by: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Vipin Sharma <vipinsh@google.com>

[PATCH v6 25/27] vfio: selftests: Add vfio_pci_liveupdate_kexec_test
Author: Vipin Sharma <vipinsh@google.com>

Add a selftest to exercise preserving a vfio-pci device in noiommu mode
across a Live Update. For now the test is simple and verifies that the
device file can be preserved, retrieved, and bound to iommufd in the new
kernel. Subsequent commits extend this test to verify that the device
cannot be opened through VFIO during the Live Update and that DMA
continues across it.

This test is added to TEST_GEN_PROGS_EXTENDED since it must be run
manually along with a kexec.

The kernel must be booted with vfio.enable_unsafe_noiommu_mode=1 and the
target device must be bound to vfio-pci in both kernels. In the new
kernel, the preserved device must be bound to vfio-pci without first
being bound to or unbound from any other driver. This can be done during
boot, e.g. with vfio-pci.ids=<vid>:<did> on the kernel command line, or
manually before running stage 2:

 $ echo vfio-pci > /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:00:04.0/driver_override
 $ echo 0000:00:04.0 > /sys/bus/pci/drivers/vfio-pci/bind

To run this test manually:

 $ ./vfio_pci_liveupdate_kexec_test -s 1 0000:00:04.0

 $ kexec ...   # NOTE: Exact method will be distro-dependent

 $ ./vfio_pci_liveupdate_kexec_test -s 2 0000:00:04.0

Do not unbind the device from vfio-pci after kexec (e.g. by running
setup.sh on a device that is already bound to vfio-pci), as unbinding it
would reset it.

This test is considered passing if all commands exit with 0.

Co-developed-by: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>
Signed-off-by: David Matlack <dmatlack@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Vipin Sharma <vipinsh@google.com>

[PATCH v6 20/27] vfio: selftests: Add iommufd noiommu mode
Author: Vipin Sharma <vipinsh@google.com>

Add an opt-in iommufd_noiommu mode (MODE_IOMMUFD_NOIOMMU) for devices
bound to vfio-pci in noiommu mode. When a device is in noiommu mode, its
VFIO character device is named /dev/vfio/devices/noiommu-vfioX (opening
it requires CAP_SYS_RAWIO), and binding and attaching to an iommufd IOAS
use a software page table.

Add __vfio_pci_get_cdev_path(bdf, noiommu) to look up either the regular
or the noiommu cdev path and return NULL when none is present, and make
vfio_pci_cdev_open() select the cdev based on mode->noiommu.

Because a device in noiommu mode DMAs directly to physical addresses,
tests that program arbitrary or unmapped IOVAs would corrupt memory.
Keep MODE_IOMMUFD_NOIOMMU out of FIXTURE_VARIANT_ADD_ALL_IOMMU_MODES()
so that only noiommu-aware tests opt into it.
