[PATCH v3 17/23] KVM: arm64: Add vcpu conduit for pkvm_hyp_req
Author: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>

The pKVM hypervisor depends on the host for a lot of operations like
memory allocation. It can raise a request by populating a pkvm_hyp_req
on the extra registers when returning a SMCCC call.

Add a new conduit, using a per-vCPU pkvm_hyp_req, populated on
ARM_EXCEPTION_PKVM_HYP_REQ. This allows processing the request later, in
a less restrictive context than the one from where the __kvm_vcpu_run
HVC is called, just like the other exceptions are handled.

Signed-off-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>

[PATCH v3 16/23] KVM: arm64: Warn on pKVM guest stage-2 block collapse
Author: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>

When pKVM is enabled, the host relies on a pkvm_mapping tree that
mirrors the actual hypervisor-owned stage-2. The hypervisor is not
allowed to silently split blocks or collapse page-tables. This should be
treated as a bug.

Therefore, WARN on guest stage-2 page-table collapse which would
otherwise be fatal anyway.

[PATCH v3 04/23] KVM: arm64: Use block-level annotations when setting
Author: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>

During pKVM initialisation, fix_host_ownership() configures the host
stage-2 mappings to match the hypervisor stage-1. Previously this was
done page-by-page. Now that the host stage-2 supports block-level
annotations, refactor the walker to apply the ownership state to an
entire range at once.

This reduces host stage-2 page-table fragmentation and memory usage by
avoiding allocation of intermediate page-table pages.

[PATCH v3 03/23] KVM: arm64: Allow block-level stage-2 annotation
Author: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>

On host to guest donation, pKVM annotates the host stage-2 invalid
entries with the GFN. Now that the host stage-2 preserves annotations on
block split, we can allow these annotations at block-level. This enables
safe support for guest stage-2 huge mappings.

Allowing block-level annotations also reduces stage-2 fragmentation when
unmapping a huge region, and reduces host stage-2 memory usage by
avoiding the allocation of intermediate page tables.

[PATCH v3 00/23] Huge mapping support for protected VMs
Author: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>

Currently, pKVM handles stage-2 mappings and ownership tracking strictly
at the PAGE_SIZE level. This series extends pKVM to support PMD_SIZE
block mappings, thereby enabling Transparent Huge Pages (THP) for
protected guests. hugetlbfs is currently unsupported as it does not
appear as swapbacked.

Summary of changes:

1. Enable host stage-2 block-level annotations:

The host stage-2 being annotated with the GFN of the guest on the
host->guest donation transitions, we allow block-level annotations to
ensure the host stage-2 mapping size matches the guest stage-2.

2. Expand ownership transition HVCs:

Enable the ownership test to work with PMD_SIZE so we can test
transitions supporting more than PAGE_SIZE.

3. Implements the PKVM_HYP_REQ_SPLIT hypervisor request:

Allows the hypervisor to ask the host to split a mapping. This ensures
the guest s2, pkvm_mapping tree and host s2 are always using the same
mapping size.

4. Wires up THP support for protected VMs:

Use a PMD_SIZE mapping for protected VMs whenever
transparent_hugepage_adjust() acknowledge the presence of a huge-mapping
in the VMM stage-1.

[PATCH v2 15/16] KVM: selftests: Add VGICv5 ITS translation cache tests
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Add end-to-end coverage for VGICv5 ITS translation and cache
invalidation. The ITS attribute test only checks the userspace API
surface; this test verifies that KVM can parse guest-provided linear
DT and ITT state and use it to deliver LPIs through guest GEN_EVENTR
and KVM_SIGNAL_MSI.

Build a two-VPE VM with IRS, ITS and an LPI IST. VPE0 programs a
linear device table and interrupt translation table, enables the
virtual ITS, enables two LPIs targeting VPE1, and sends GEN_EVENTR to
populate the translation cache with EventID to LPI A. VPE1 reports the
LPI identity it consumed.

VPE0 then rewrites the ITT to point at LPI B without invalidation, and
a host KVM_SIGNAL_MSI injection verifies that the stale cached LPI A
translation is reused. VPE0 invalidates the event with ITS_INV_EVENTR
and the host verifies that LPI B is delivered.

VPE0 then rewrites the ITT back to LPI A, invalidates the device with
ITS_INV_DEVICER, and the host verifies that LPI A is delivered again.
This exercises the guest-side event and device invalidation paths
rather than invalidating translations directly from the test host.

Also cover negative MSI paths by checking that KVM_SIGNAL_MSI rejects a
message without KVM_MSI_VALID_DEVID and rejects a message whose address
does not target the GICv5 ITS. Finally, inject once before guest-side
ITS disable to prove delivery still works, then have VPE0 disable the
ITS and verify that a cached KVM_SIGNAL_MSI delivery is rejected with
ENXIO.

Check 32-bit accesses to both halves of DT_BASER and preservation of
the untouched half. Verify that guest writes to the ITS ID registers
and READ_EVENT_DATAR are ignored, and that READ_EVENT_DATAR can be read
at both 32-bit and 64-bit widths.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---

[PATCH v2 13/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Implement irq forwarding
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

GICv5, like GICv4, supports direct injection of interrupts via the
ITS. Move the existing GICv4 interface behind a common shim that selects
the appropriate implementation. For GICv5 forwarding, the ITS driver
replaces a host ITTE with one targeting a guest. The host no longer
receives that interrupt until forwarding is removed and the host ITTE is
restored.

A guest can make multiple DeviceID/EventID pairs resolve to the same
LPI, even though it should not. Track the cache entry responsible for
direct injection, and let only that entry tear it down during guest
invalidation. Explicitly unforwarding an IRQ clears the owner without
removing the cached translation.

The ITS driver writes the virtual ITTE before invalidating its cache.
If invalidation times out, attempt to restore the host ITTE before
falling back to software injection. If the restoration also times out,
keep the host IRQ association and a separate LPI reference so
unforwarding can be retried even after the guest invalidates the
translation. Clear KVM's forwarding state only after the host ITTE has
been restored.

Forwarding can be set up from another process's address space through
IRQ bypass. A cache miss in this path must still walk the guest's DT and
ITT. Read the entries from the VM's mm using access_remote_vm() when
the caller does not have that mm. Hold an mm reference and protect the
memslot lookup with SRCU, and fail the lookup on a short read.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---

[PATCH v2 11/16] irqchip/gic-v5: Enable direct injection in ITS
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Allow KVM to replace a host ITTE with a virtual translation that
directly injects an interrupt into a guest. Add an irq_chip callback to
install and remove the virtual translation, and disable lazy masking
while the interrupt is forwarded. The KVM caller is added later.

The ITS writes the ITTE before invalidating its cache. If invalidation
times out, hardware may still use the virtual translation even though
setup failed. Allow disable to restore the host ITTE when the forwarded
flag is clear, and retain retryable state if restoration also times
out. Keep lazy masking disabled until the host mapping is restored.

Co-authored-by: Timothy Hayes <timothy.hayes@arm.com>
Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 10/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS userspace register access
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Userspace needs access to the emulated GICv5 ITS MMIO state in order
to inspect and restore the device state around VM reset and migration.
Expose the GICv5 ITS register frame through KVM_DEV_ARM_VGIC_GRP_ITS_REGS.

Reuse the same MMIO register descriptors used by the guest-facing
IODEV, but apply the usual userspace access constraints: the ITS base
address must have been configured, the register offset must map to an
emulated ITS register, and userspace accesses must use the natural
32-bit or 64-bit register alignment.

Add control handling for KVM_DEV_ARM_ITS_CTRL_RESET. The reset is
performed while all vCPUs are stopped and while holding the ITS lock,
so it cannot race with guest MMIO updates or translation lookups. The
reset clears guest-programmed state and cached translations while
preserving the advertised ITS capability defaults.

Do not expose GICv5 ITS SAVE_TABLES or RESTORE_TABLES controls. For
GICv5, the guest-owned DT and ITTs are the authoritative translation
state, and the KVM translation cache is disposable state that can be
rebuilt from guest memory.

Restore guest read-only IDR0-IDR2 through dedicated userspace writes,
validating the advertised capabilities before changing them. Also
allow userspace to restore READ_EVENT_DATAR while keeping guest writes
to that result register ignored.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---

 1 file changed, 322 insertions(+), 49 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 09/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS MSI injection
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Now that KVM can resolve GICv5 ITS translations from guest memory,
allow userspace and irqfd MSI injection to target a virtual GICv5 ITS.

Add helpers to validate that an MSI address resolves to a GICv5 ITS
IODEV, find the ITS for an MSI, resolve the DeviceID/EventID pair to a
vgic_irq, and inject the resulting LPI with the GICv5 VDPEND path.
GICv5 MSIs require KVM_MSI_VALID_DEVID, as the DeviceID is part of the
ITS translation key.

Although MSI data is 32 bits, GICv5 supports only 16-bit EventIDs.
Reject a nonzero upper half before narrowing the EventID for cache
lookups, so malformed MSI data cannot alias a valid translation.

Provide both the slow path, which resolves the translation from guest
memory and populates the ITS cache, and the inatomic cached path used
by irqfd. If the cached path misses, irqfd retries in a work item. The
worker borrows the VM's mm while walking the guest tables, then drops
it before injecting the LPI.

Wire kvm_set_msi() and kvm_arch_set_irq_inatomic() to use the GICv5
ITS injection helpers for GICv5 VMs, while keeping the existing GICv3
ITS path unchanged for GICv3 VMs.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 08/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS translation cache maintenance
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Walking the guest DT and ITTs on every MSI or ITS event is unnecessary
once a DeviceID/EventID pair has been resolved. Add an XArray indexed
by the DeviceID/EventID pair to cache resolved translations as a
disposable acceleration structure, avoiding repeated walks of guest
memory.

Cache entries hold references to the resolved struct vgic_irq objects
stored in the VM-wide LPI xarray. Lookups use RCU to access entries in
interrupt context without taking a raw spinlock, and take an IRQ
reference before returning an entry to the caller. Cache eviction drops
the reference held by the cache and defers freeing the entry until RCU
readers have finished. This keeps the cache tied to the normal LPI
object lifetime instead of storing raw LPI numbers.

Invalidate cached translations when the table configuration or guest
invalidation commands make them stale. Changes to DT_BASER or DT_CFGR
drop the entire cache, while writes of unchanged values leave it intact.
INV_EVENTR removes either one event or the events covered by an L1 ITT
entry, and INV_DEVICER removes the translations covered by either an L2
DT entry or an L1 DT entry.

The cache is not part of any userspace ABI and is never authoritative:
entries can always be rebuilt from guest memory on the next lookup.

The vgic_irq objects for LPIs track valid translations rather than
mirror interrupt state. Mirroring that state would require querying
the GIC on every guest exit. Once direct injection is added, the
objects also record whether an LPI is mapped into the host ITT.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/vgic/vgic-its-v5.c | 219 ++++++++++++++++++++++++++++--
 include/kvm/arm_vgic.h            |   3 +
 2 files changed, 208 insertions(+), 14 deletions(-)

[PATCH v2 07/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS guest table translation emulation
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

The GICv5 ITS consumes guest-owned translation tables. Unlike the
GICv3 ITS, KVM does not maintain a separate software device/table
model for GICv5 translations; the guest's DT and ITTs are the
authoritative source of DeviceID/EventID to LPI mappings.

Add helpers to walk both linear and two-level guest Device Tables and
Interrupt Translation Tables. The walker reads the guest table entries
under the ITS lock, validates the relevant valid bits and range fields,
and resolves a DeviceID/EventID pair to the LPI encoded in the guest's
ITTE.

Use the table walker to implement the translation side effects of the
MMIO interface. READ_EVENTR now resolves DIDR/EIDR and reports the LPI
through READ_EVENT_DATAR, while GEN_EVENTR resolves GEN_EVENT_DIDR and
GEN_EVENT_EIDR and injects the resulting LPI into the VM.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 06/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS MMIO state emulation
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

In order to expose a virtual GICv5 ITS to the guest, KVM needs to
emulate the ITS CONFIG_FRAME and its translation-control registers.
Replace the placeholder ITS IODEV region with the GICv5 ITS register
layout and handlers for the architected MMIO state.

Provide read/write emulation for the ITS ID registers, IIDR, AIDR,
CR0, CR1, DT_BASER, DT_CFGR, DIDR, EIDR, invalidation registers,
READ_EVENT, GEN_EVENT, status registers, and the RAZ/WI MMIO
registers that KVM does not implement. Reuse the shared CoreSight
identification-register helper so the ITS reports the same component
identification values as the IRS.

At this stage the translation-triggering registers only record state.
Walking the guest's DT/ITT, generating READ_EVENT results, and
injecting GEN_EVENT LPIs are added by later commits.

[PATCH v2 05/16] KVM: arm64: gic-v5: Add ITS device skeleton
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Introduce the basic KVM device plumbing for a GICv5 ITS. This adds the
GICv5 ITS device type and address attribute, registers the device ops,
and wires a GICv5 ITS IODEV into the generic VGIC MMIO dispatch path.

Add struct vgic_v5_its to hold the ITS base address, the IODEV plumbing,
the backing KVM device pointer, and the general ITS mutex. Allocate this
state from the KVM device create path, initialise the mutex and base
address, and mark the VM as having an ITS. GICv5 MSIs require a DeviceID,
so set msis_require_devid when the ITS is created.

Record the VGIC model required by an ITS and reject mismatched device
creation in either order. Check GICv3 ITS creation as well, so the two
ITS models cannot be combined in one VM.

The MMIO region is registered once userspace provides the ITS base
address through KVM_VGIC_V5_ADDR_TYPE_ITS. For now the IODEV only
provides a RAZ/WI placeholder region; the actual ITS register emulation
is added separately.

[PATCH v2 01/16] KVM: arm64: vgic: Split out common LPI allocation
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

The GICv3 ITS code currently owns vgic_add_lpi(), even though the
first half of the helper is not ITS specific. It allocates or finds
the VM-wide vgic_irq object in lpi_xa and initialises the common LPI
state before the GICv3 ITS code reads the property table and pending
state.

GICv5 needs to reuse the same LPI object lifetime and refcounting, but
without reading the GICv3 property table. Move the allocation and
xarray insertion path into vgic_alloc_lpi() in vgic.c. Both
vgic_add_lpi() and the GICv3 ITS wrapper perform an initial lookup,
then call this helper on a miss. The ITS wrapper continues to load the
property and pending state, while GICv5 can use the common allocator
without GICv3 setup.

Keeping the lookup outside vgic_alloc_lpi() preserves GICv3 behaviour
when another caller inserts the LPI during allocation: the ITS wrapper
still performs its setup on the returned LPI. A second lookup inside
the allocator could instead treat it as already initialised and skip
that setup.

Initialise LPIs as Group 1 before publishing them in lpi_xa. GICv3
LPIs are Group 1, and another vCPU can find a new LPI as soon as it is
published. GICv5 does not use the group field.

This preserves the intended GICv3 behaviour while making the common
LPI storage usable by other VGIC users.

Signed-off-by: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

[PATCH v2 00/16] KVM: arm64: Add GICv5 ITS support
Author: Sascha Bischoff <sascha.bischoff@arm.com>

Hi all,

This is the second posting of my GICv5 KVM ITS series.

This series builds on the GICv5 IRS support now in 7.4-rc1 and adds
support for the GICv5 ITS, as described by the GICv5 specification
[1]. It allows a GICv5 guest to receive LPIs generated by MSIs,
including those from physical PCI devices mapped into the guest via
VFIO.

The ITS is exposed as a separate KVM device, alongside the VGICv5
device. The guest owns the device table and interrupt translation
tables. KVM walks these tables to resolve a DeviceID and EventID to an
LPI, and caches the result until the guest invalidates the relevant
translation or changes the table configuration.

The main pieces are:

* An emulated GICv5 ITS CONFIG_FRAME, including table configuration,
  event generation, and invalidation registers.

* Guest table walking for linear and two-level device and interrupt
  translation tables, together with a disposable XArray-based
  translation cache.

* MSI injection through KVM_SIGNAL_MSI and irqfd. GICv5 requires a
  DeviceID for each MSI, and the virtual ITS supports 16-bit EventIDs.

* Direct injection through the host ITS when an interrupt is forwarded
  to a vCPU. Forwarding teardown restores the host translation.

* Userspace attributes to configure the ITS address, access its
  registers, and reset the emulated state, together with ABI
  documentation.

* Selftests for the ITS attributes, guest table translation, MSI
  delivery, and guest-driven cache invalidation.

The guest tables remain the authoritative translation state. KVM's
cache can be discarded and rebuilt from guest memory; there is no
separate ITS table data for the VMM to save/restore.

Lorenzo's GICv5 Linux software enablement guide [2] describes how to
set up the GICv5 software stack. These changes have been tested on an
Arm FVP 11.32 release [3].

These changes are based on v7.4-rc1. The corresponding kvmtool patches
have been posted separately [4]. I plan to post an updated kvmtool
series shortly.

These changes are also available as a branch at [5]. For reference, v1
of this series can be found at [6].

This series applies independently of the host GICv5 fixes [7], but
concurrent use may encounter the host driver issues described there. A
branch with both series is available at [8]. Both series need to be
merged for the ITS support to be complete.

The main changes since v1 are:

* Rebased onto v7.4-rc1.

* Reused upstream ITS model tracking, removing the separate has_its
  state.

* Sashiko flagged some races for GICv3 LPIs, which were caused by me
  making the allocation common to both vITSes. Group 1 is set before
  adding the LPI to the tracking XArray. The GICv3 ITS path performs
  one initial lookup before allocating a new LPI.

* Replaced the translation cache hash table with an XArray. This
  avoids the locking/lockup issues raised by Sashiko. Furthermore, it
  is no longer possible for a malicious guest to force all LPI
  mappings into one hashtable bucket.

* Tightened translation and MSI handling: enforce advertised DeviceID
  and EventID limits, report invalid guest translations as blocked
  MSIs, and allow software injection to proceed if caching fails.

* Expanded ITS register handling and the userspace ABI: serialise
  register access, support 32-bit accesses to 64-bit registers, and
  validate restoration of IDR0-IDR2 and READ_EVENT_DATAR while keeping
  them read-only to the guest.

* Hardened direct injection teardown: track which translation owns
  forwarding, synchronise host translation restoration, and retain the
  IRQ association when a timeout requires a later retry.

* Extended selftests for register access widths, read-only behaviour,
  ID limits, malformed MSIs, and cache invalidation.

Thanks for taking the time to look at these changes!

Thanks,
Sascha

[PATCH v3 2/2] KVM: arm64: Support FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 in host handler
Author: Snehal Koukuntla <snehalreddy@google.com>

FF-A v1.3 BET0 (DEN0140 rev K) adds the FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 interface,
allowing an Owner to reclaim a subset of pages from a shared or lent
memory region by populating constituent memory region descriptors in
its TX buffer along with a starting descriptor byte offset and initial
page index. Upon completion, the Relayer returns the number of 4K pages
reclaimed in x3.

Support FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 in the host FF-A proxy handler. Validate the
register arguments, copy the constituent descriptors from the host TX
buffer into the hypervisor's TX buffer, and check the descriptor bounds,
the total page count and the initial page index on that copy before
forwarding the call to the SPMD. On success, unshare the reclaimed page
ranges from the host stage-2 page tables.

On a Pixel board (pKVM, 16K pages, EL3 SPMC), reclaiming a single 16K
range drops from 1.3us to 0.7us (~45% faster) by avoiding
FFA_MEM_RETRIEVE_REQ/RX_RELEASE (~0.6us), and single-call
FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 stays faster across small range counts (1.3us vs 1.9us
at 16 ranges; 2 calls break even). Up to 64MiB (4096 ranges), batching
256 descriptors/call adds 15 preemption points, capping each EL2/EL3
call at ~11us while keeping total SMC time within 5%.

Link: https://support.arm.com/documentation/den0140/k
Reviewed-by: Vincent Donnefort <vdonnefort@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Snehal Koukuntla <snehalreddy@google.com>
---
Changes in v3:
- Drop RFC tag and update specification reference to FF-A v1.3 BET0
  (DEN0140 rev K)
- Add benchmark numbers to commit description
- Add Vincent's Reviewed-by tag (no code changes)

Changes in v2:
- Rename FFA_FN64_MEM_RECLAIM2 to FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 (sashiko)
- Check that the sum of pg_cnt across all constituent descriptors
  matches total_pages, guarding against overflow (Vincent)
- Simplify the flags and offset alignment checks (Vincent)

[PATCH v3 0/2] KVM: arm64: Support FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 in host handler
Author: Snehal Koukuntla <snehalreddy@google.com>

The Arm Firmware Framework for Arm A-profile (FF-A) v1.3 BET0
specification (DEN0140 rev K) [1] introduces the optional
FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 (0xC4000091) ABI.

Reclaiming large memory regions with the existing FFA_MEM_RECLAIM ABI
can take significant time, or relies on a stateful time-slicing
mechanism in the Relayer. FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 allows an Owner to reclaim
exclusive access to a subset of pages from a shared or lent memory
region by passing an array of constituent memory region descriptors in
its TX buffer along with a starting descriptor byte offset and initial
page index. The Relayer returns the number of 4K pages reclaimed in
that invocation without maintaining intermediate transaction state.

On a Pixel board (pKVM, 16K pages, EL3 SPMC), reclaiming a single 16K
range drops from 1.3us to 0.7us (~45% faster) by avoiding
FFA_MEM_RETRIEVE_REQ/RX_RELEASE (~0.6us), and single-call
FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 stays faster across small range counts (1.3us vs 1.9us
at 16 ranges; 2 calls break even). Up to 64MiB (4096 ranges), batching
256 descriptors/call adds 15 preemption points, capping each EL2/EL3
call at ~11us while keeping total SMC time within 5%.

This series adds the FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 function ID definition and
implements host proxy support in the pKVM nVHE FF-A handler, unsharing
the reclaimed pages in the host stage-2 page tables upon a successful
reclaim response from EL3/SPMD.

With the FF-A v1.3 specification now at BETA (1.3 BET0), drop the RFC
tag so the series can be considered for merging.

This series is based on v7.4-rc1.

Changes in v3:
- Drop RFC tag now that FF-A v1.3 BET0 (DEN0140 rev K) is released
- Update specification references and links to DEN0140 rev K (1.3 BET0)
- Add benchmark numbers to the cover letter and Patch 2 commit message
- Collect Vincent's Reviewed-by on Patch 2
- Rebase on v7.4-rc1
- Link to v2: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261016082341.4030770-1-snehalreddy@google.com

Changes in v2:
- Mark as RFC until the FF-A v1.3 specification reaches BETA (Sudeep)
- Rename FFA_FN64_MEM_RECLAIM2 to FFA_MEM_RECLAIM2 (sashiko)
- Check total_pages against the sum of constituent pg_cnt (Vincent)
- Simplify the flags and offset alignment checks (Vincent)
- Rebase on latest kvmarm/next
- Link to v1: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20260921164748.2958578-1-snehalreddy@google.com

[1] https://support.arm.com/documentation/den0140/k

[PATCH 10/11] KVM: arm64: CCA: Support runtime faulting of memory
Author: Steven Price <steven.price@arm.com>

At runtime if the realm guest accesses memory which hasn't yet been
mapped then KVM needs to either populate the region or fault the guest.

For memory in the lower (protected) region of IPA, verify that the
corresponding guest_memfd page is private before delegating to the RMM.
The RMM then maps the page into the realm and initializes its contents
to zero.

For memory in the upper (shared) IPA region, map the backing page as
non-secure memory. Check that a guest_memfd page is shared when the
fault uses guest_memfd; ordinary userspace-backed memslots follow the
normal user memory fault path.

If the fault IPA and guest_memfd attributes disagree then exit to
userspace with KVM_EXIT_MEMORY_FAULT so the VMM can perform the required
conversion.

Co-developed-by: Suzuki K Poulose <suzuki.poulose@arm.com>
Signed-off-by: Suzuki K Poulose <suzuki.poulose@arm.com>
Signed-off-by: Steven Price <steven.price@arm.com>
---

[PATCH 11/11] KVM: arm64: CCA: Add Realm stage-2 MMU operations
Author: Steven Price <steven.price@arm.com>


Register the Realm stage-2 MMU operations now that the RMM mapping
and unmapping helpers are available. Route unmapping through the
RMM, and use the existing fault handler for Realm memory faults.

The RMM handles TLB invalidation when stage-2 mappings change, so
the host has no separate TLB flush to perform. Realm stage-2 does
not support access flags, including for shared IPAs.

[PATCH 07/11] KVM: arm64: CCA: Tear down RTTs
Author: Steven Price <steven.price@arm.com>

The RMM owns the stage 2 page tables for a realm, and KVM must request
that the RMM creates/destroys entries as necessary. The physical pages
to store the page tables are delegated to the realm as required, and can
be undelegated when no longer used.

Creating new RTTs is the easy part, tearing down is a little more
tricky. The result of realm_rtt_destroy() can be used to effectively
walk the tree and destroy the entries (undelegating pages that were
given to the realm).

Unmap the Realm IPA space once during stage-2 teardown. Defer destruction
of non-root RTTs until final Realm destruction, after REALM_TERMINATE and
before destroying the RD; root RTTs remain until the RD has gone away.

Signed-off-by: Steven Price <steven.price@arm.com>
---

[PATCH 00/11] KVM: arm64: CCA: Prepare Realm memory management
Author: Steven Price <steven.price@arm.com>

This series adds the memory plumbing for CCA building on the basic
plumbing[1] that has just been merged.

These patches add the internal realm VM state and RMM feature checks,
prepare realm stage-2 setup and teardown, and provide RTT mapping and
invalidation helpers. Realm memslots are now contrained to the lower
half of the IPA space, because faults treat the protected and shared
halves as aliasing. The final patch then connects these helpers to the
KVM stage-2 MMU operations.

includes the realm descriptor creation, REC entry/exit, activation and
the new uAPI. This series by itself is also available as a git
branch[3]. However the new code isn't generally active without the full
integration branch.

[PATCH v3 12/13] KVM: arm64: Reject ITS commands whose IDs are out of range
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

The ITS can't be relied on to reject a command with an out-of-range ID:
IHI 0069H.b 5.3.2 makes the response to a command error CONSTRAINED
UNPREDICTABLE, and one permitted outcome is "The data that generated the
error or errors is treated as having a legal value, and the command is
processed accordingly". An out-of-range DeviceID, EventID or ICID then
has the ITS access memory past the device table, the ITT or the
collection table.

Bound each ID before forwarding the command: the DeviceID by
GITS_TYPER.Devbits and the device table, the EventID by the size its
device was mapped with, and the ICID by the ICID width and the number of
collections. Also reject a command for an event of a device whose unmap
is queued, as its ITE would land in an ITT the hypervisor may already
have released. The driver loses nothing. It never allocates an
out-of-range DeviceID or EventID, or sends a command for a device after
its unmap. Its ICIDs are CPU numbers, which exceed the collections only
on an ITS that already treats its MAPC as a command error (IHI 0069H.b
5.3.9).

MAPTI's pINTID, which the ITS only stores in the ITE, and the RDbase of
MAPC, MOVALL and SYNC are left to the redistributor series, as both
concern the redistributors.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/hyp/nvhe/its_emulate.c | 49 +++++++++++++++++++++++++--
 include/linux/irqchip/arm-gic-v3.h    |  3 ++
 2 files changed, 49 insertions(+), 3 deletions(-)

[PATCH v3 11/13] KVM: arm64: Take the ITT for the lifetime of a device mapping
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Once a device is mapped, the ITS writes its ITT on every MAPTI and reads
it on every translation, indexing the collection and vPE tables with
whatever ICID and vPEID the ITE holds (IHI 0069H.b B.7). As "The format
of the ITT entries is IMPLEMENTATION DEFINED" (5.3.10), the hypervisor
can't check an ITT the host can write.

On a MAPD with V == 1, the hypervisor takes the ITT's pages and zeroes
them before forwarding the command, and rejects the MAPD if the DeviceID
is already mapped, the ITT isn't page-aligned or Size exceeds
GITS_TYPER.ID_bits. It sizes the ITT from the MAPD's Size and
GITS_TYPER.ITT_entry_size, except on QDF2400 rev 1, whose ITS writes
16-byte entries whatever ITT_entry_size holds (erratum 0065). The ITTs
of the devices mapped before the takeover, which the driver lists, are
taken as they stand.

A MAPD with V == 0 gives the pages back once the hypervisor has seen the
ITS consume it, on a GITS_CREADR read or a GITS_CWRITER or GITS_CTLR
write. The driver's unmap polls GITS_CREADR before freeing the ITT, so
the pages are back by then, unless the poll times out on a stalled ITS.
The host's next access to the pages it freed then aborts.

A GITS_CBASER write resets the queue, so it now aborts until the ITS has
consumed every forwarded command, as a dropped MAPD would leave a device
tracked that the ITS never mapped. The driver writes GITS_CBASER only on
resume, after its waits, so a resume aborts only if a wait timed out
before suspend.

The hypervisor tracks a fixed number of devices per ITS and returns that
number from the setup call. its_create_device() fails a device past it
rather than queue a MAPD the hypervisor would reject.

Co-developed-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Co-developed-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v2 (the ITT half of v2 patch 08):
    - The ITT is donated to the hypervisor on a MAPD, zeroed and
      cleaned, and given back after the unmap (was shared and pinned, so
      the host could still write it)
    - The ITT pages go back only once the ITS is seen past the unmap
    - A GITS_CBASER write aborts until the ITS has consumed every
      command forwarded
    - The ITT must be page-aligned, where v2 under-counted the pages of
      one starting mid-page (sashiko)
    - The page count uses 16-byte entries on QDF2400 rev 1 (erratum
      0065)
    - A MAPD V=1 for a DeviceID already mapped is rejected, where v2
      shared the ITT a second time (sashiko)
    - One tracker entry per device, in a sorted array, instead of one
      per page keyed on the pfn (sashiko)
    - The devices mapped before the takeover are taken at setup (v2
      never tracked them)
    - A MAPD V=0 for a DeviceID that isn't mapped is forwarded once its
      level-1 entry is valid (was -EINVAL)
    - its_create_device() fails a device past the tracker's capacity,
      which setup returns

[PATCH v3 10/13] KVM: arm64: Take the ITS tables and restrict GITS_BASER<n>
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

The ITS writes device and collection entries into the tables
GITS_BASER<n> points to. At setup the hypervisor takes every such table
and every level-2 page an indirect device table already points to. That
includes the vPE table, which the driver allocates whenever the ITS has
one. With GICv4 off for the host, only the device table gains level-2
pages after setup.

From the takeover on, the driver writes new level-1 entries into a copy
of the device table, which the hypervisor pins. On the first MAPD that
needs a new entry, the hypervisor publishes the copy's entry if it holds
only Valid and the address of an aligned level-2 page, and takes and
zeroes that page. A valid hardware entry never changes, and a MAPD whose
entry stays invalid is rejected, as the ITS would discard its write. So
is a MAPD past the end of a flat device table.

GITS_BASER<n> writes now go through only to a register that pointed to
a table at setup, with the ITS disabled and quiescent. The hypervisor
then writes back the value it read at setup, and an enable aborts unless
every such register still holds it.

Co-developed-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v2 (v2 patches 07 and 12, and the level-1 half of 08):
    - The tables come from GITS_BASER<n> as the hypervisor reads them,
      not from a host-supplied address, value, order and page size
      (sashiko)
    - A level-1 entry is write-once: only Valid 0 -> 1 is published (was
      any Valid flip, the page given back on 1 -> 0)
    - A published entry is read once and must name an aligned level-2
      page, which is zeroed, and cleaned for a non-shareable table
      (sashiko)
    - Level-2 pages are taken as DIV_ROUND_UP(psz, PAGE_SIZE) kernel
      pages, where v2's psz >> PAGE_SHIFT was 0 below PAGE_SIZE
      (sashiko)
    - Level-1 entries are read and written as __le64 (sashiko)
    - Page_Size, Size, Entry_Size and the 64K address encoding are
      decoded per the architecture (v2 didn't fold PA[51:48])
    - Only the device table has a level-1 copy (was a copy of every
      table)
    - A vPE table that GICv4.1 ITSes share is taken once (was a second
      donation, which fails)
    - A MAPD whose level-1 entry stays invalid is rejected
    - A GITS_BASER<n> write stores the value read at setup, whole,
      rather than the host's value under a mask (sashiko)
    - A GITS_BASER<n> write to a register that named no table, or while
      the ITS is enabled or not quiescent, aborts (was a silent drop)
    - An enable also aborts unless every GITS_BASER<n> that named a
      table still holds its value

[PATCH v3 09/13] KVM: arm64: Emulate the ITS command queue
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>


The ITS writes to the memory its commands point to, so a host that
queues commands freely can aim those writes at the hypervisor or a
protected guest.

Register each ITS's control frame with the hypervisor, which takes
ownership of it at initialisation, so that every host access to it traps
once the host is deprivileged. GITS_TRANSLATER is in the next frame,
which stays host-mapped. Each ITS takes one of the eight region slots,
so KVM doesn't initialise in protected mode with more than eight ITSes.
Inside the stop_machine() callback that deprivileges the host, the
hypervisor reads GITS_CBASER, GITS_CWRITER and GITS_CREADR itself, takes
the queue and pins the driver's copy. If that fails, no CPU is
deprivileged and KVM_CREATE_VM fails with -ENODEV.

On a GITS_CWRITER write, the hypervisor reads each new command once from
the driver's copy of the queue, checks it and copies it into the
hardware queue. A rejected command aborts the host's store, after the
commands before it have been forwarded. For now that's a vLPI command or
an unknown encoding, neither of which the driver queues.

An access without a valid syndrome aborts. Otherwise, reads go to the
hardware. Writes abort, except to GITS_CWRITER, to GITS_BASER<n> until
the next patch, and to GITS_CTLR and GITS_CBASER, which the driver
writes on suspend and resume. A GITS_CBASER write goes through only with
the ITS disabled and quiescent, and the hypervisor writes back the value
it read at setup. An enable aborts unless GITS_CBASER holds that value.
It's dropped rather than aborted while the ITS isn't quiescent (IHI
0069H.b 12.19.4), as the driver writes one from a syscore callback when
a quiesce times out, and an abort there would take the host down.

The takeover takes cpus_read_lock() before the driver's prepare, as
drivers take dev_alloc_lock through the MSI layer with it held.

Signed-off-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Co-developed-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Co-developed-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v2 (v2 patches 04, 06, 10, 11 and 13, merged):
    - The takeover runs in the stop_machine() callback that
      deprivileges the host, while every CPU is still privileged (was
      after the stage-2 install)
    - A failed takeover deprivileges no CPU, and KVM_CREATE_VM then
      returns -ENODEV
    - The hypervisor reads GITS_CBASER, GITS_CWRITER and GITS_CREADR
      itself, rather than taking the host's queue and length
    - Every rejected access aborts, a rejected command once the commands
      before it are forwarded (v2 dropped it and skipped the
      instruction)
    - Commands are forwarded and never rolled back: the completion poll,
      the -EIO/-EBUSY split and the rollback are gone (sashiko)
    - The vLPI commands and unknown encodings are rejected (were
      forwarded)
    - A write to a register the emulation doesn't handle aborts, where
      v2 forwarded it (sashiko)
    - Accesses are decoded by offset and size, where v2's handlers
      ignored the offset within a register (sashiko)
    - A GITS_CBASER write stores the value read at setup, whole, where
      v2 let the host's cacheability and shareability through (sashiko)
    - A GITS_CBASER write while the ITS is enabled or not quiescent
      aborts, and one that goes through resets the hypervisor's side of
      the queue
    - An enable aborts unless GITS_CBASER still holds the value read at
      setup
    - GITS_CWRITER's offset is bounded by the queue size, and a write
      that would overrun GITS_CREADR aborts
    - Every forwarded command is cleaned to PoC, where v2's test for it
      ignored inner cacheability (sashiko)
    - The trap handler, from v2 patch 03, calls the ITS emulation
      directly (was a per-region callback)
    - The written value is masked to the access size, and a read is
      sign-extended per ESR_EL2.SSE and sized per SF (sashiko)
    - The trap path tests num_protected_reg first and no longer calls
      addr_is_memory(), and FAR_TO_FIPA_OFFSET() replaces v2's FAR_MASK
    - KVM registers each ITS from the driver's its_pkvm_iter_its(), at
      KVM init (was a DT walk)

[PATCH v3 08/13] irqchip/gic-v3-its: Add the protected-KVM takeover entry points
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>


Under protected KVM the hypervisor takes the memory the ITS reads its
command queue and tables from. From the takeover on, the driver writes
its commands and level-1 device table entries into copies that the
hypervisor checks before the ITS sees them.

Add the driver's side of the takeover KVM runs at deprivilege, in three
steps. its_pkvm_setup_prepare() allocates the copies and the pages the
hypervisor keeps its state in, and lists the devices already mapped,
with their ITTs, as the ITS's own record of them is IMPLEMENTATION
DEFINED. It holds every ITS's dev_alloc_lock, which device creation and
teardown already take, until its_pkvm_setup_finish(), so the list stays
exact.

its_pkvm_setup_commit() runs with every CPU stopped, and switches the
driver to its copies only once the hypervisor has taken every ITS, so a
failure leaves the driver on the hardware queues. If the switch didn't
run, finish frees the copies and the state pages. After a failed commit,
that includes the state of an ITS the hypervisor took, which the host
can still use as no CPU is deprivileged.

its_pkvm_iter_its() gives KVM each ITS's base, to register the control
frames before the hypervisor initialises. Nothing calls these yet.

Signed-off-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Co-developed-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Co-developed-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v2 (v2 patch 05):
    - Prepare, commit and finish entry points replace the lock pair KVM
      held across on_each_cpu() with IRQs off (sashiko)
    - Prepare does every allocation before stop_machine(), with
      GFP_KERNEL rather than GFP_ATOMIC under raw spinlocks (sashiko)
    - Commit runs in the deprivilege's stop_machine() callback, and the
      driver switches to its copies only once every ITS is taken
      (sashiko)
    - Every ITS's dev_alloc_lock is held from prepare to finish
    - The descriptor names memory only: the state pages, the queue copy
      and the devices already mapped (was register values and its_baser)

[PATCH v3 07/13] KVM: arm64: Deprivilege the host under stop_machine()
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

pkvm_drop_host_privileges() installs the host stage 2 on every CPU with
on_each_cpu(), and each CPU goes back to running the host as soon as its
own __pkvm_prot_finalize() returns. The ITS takeover a later patch adds
has to run while every other CPU is stopped and still privileged, and
let them finalise only after it. Stopping them means no CPU can post an
ITS command while the driver switches to its copies of the queue and
tables. Keeping them privileged means a takeover that fails leaves the
host as it was.

Finalise from a stop_machine() callback instead: no CPU leaves it until
every CPU's callback has returned. An error from any CPU still reaches
finalize_pkvm(), which runs once at boot.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/pkvm.c | 13 ++++---------
 1 file changed, 4 insertions(+), 9 deletions(-)

[PATCH v3 06/13] KVM: arm64: Share the ITS command field accessors with the hypervisor
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

The vgic's ITS emulation reads command fields with its_cmd_mask_field()
and the its_cmd_get_*() accessors. struct its_cmd_block, the block they
decode, is private to the ITS driver, and a later patch has the
protected KVM hypervisor parse the host's commands too.

Move the command block and the accessors to arm-gic-v3.h, which already
holds the GITS_CMD_* encodings, so there is one copy of each. Only KVM
reads commands, but the accessors decode the block the driver writes, so
they sit with it rather than in a KVM header.

No functional change intended.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v3 05/13] irqchip/gic-v3-its: Compute the wait target under the ITS lock
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

its_wait_for_range_completion() converts the posted command pointer into
a queue offset against its->cmd_base, after the caller has dropped
its->lock. That's fine while cmd_base never changes, but the protected
KVM takeover in a later patch switches cmd_base to a copy of the queue
under the lock, and a wait racing with it would compute its target
against the wrong base.

Compute the offset in its_post_commands(), under the lock, and pass it
to the wait instead of the pointer.

No functional change intended.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v3 04/13] irqchip/gic-v3-its: Page-align ITT allocations under protected KVM
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>


A later patch has the protected KVM hypervisor take each device's ITT
for as long as the device is mapped, by having the host donate its
pages. A page the hypervisor already owns can't be donated again, so the
MAPD of a device whose ITT shares a page with another device's would be
rejected.

Round each ITT up to whole pages in protected mode. This spends up to a
page per MSI device where a 256-byte ITT would have shared one: tens of
pages on a phone, thousands on a server with thousands of VFs.

Signed-off-by: Bartłomiej Grzesik <bgrzesik@google.com>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

[PATCH v3 03/13] irqchip/gic-v3: Disable GICv4 features in protected KVM mode
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>


Later patches have the protected KVM hypervisor take the ITS from the
host and check each command the host queues. They don't emulate the
vLPI commands, as VMAPP's VPT_base, for one, is a host-chosen address
the ITS and the redistributors write to.

Clear has_vlpis and has_rvpeid at redistributor probe in protected mode,
so the host never issues a vLPI command, and print that GICv4 is off.
The host loses GICv4 direct injection for its non-protected guests,
which is off unless kvm-arm.vgic_v4_enable is set, and can't restore a
guest saved with GICD_TYPER2.nASSGIcap set.

The test is kvm_get_mode(), as the redistributors are probed before the
cpucaps are final. It lives in irq-gic-common.h as
gic_is_protected_kvm(), so that the ITS driver can use it too and
linux/kvm_host.h stays out of the rest of irqchip.

[PATCH v3 02/13] KVM: arm64: Take host-registered MMIO regions at pKVM finalisation
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>


To mediate a device, the protected KVM hypervisor has to own its
registers, so that a host access to them takes a stage-2 abort rather
than reaching the device.

Let the host register MMIO regions before the hypervisor initialises,
and have __pkvm_init_finalise() donate each one and record where it's
mapped in the hypervisor's private range. The donation runs before
pkvm_check_host_ownership(), which then covers those mappings too. A
failed donation fails the hypervisor's initialisation before the host
stage 2 is installed, so nothing is given back.

Nothing registers a region yet.

Signed-off-by: Sebastian Ene <sebastianene@google.com>
Co-developed-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

    - The trap handling moved to patch 9, which calls the ITS emulation
      directly (was a per-region callback)
    - Each region records its hypervisor VA
    - A failed donation is no longer unwound

[PATCH v3 01/13] KVM: arm64: Donate MMIO to the hypervisor
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>


Add __pkvm_host_donate_hyp_mmio() so IOMMU drivers in the hypervisor can
take ownership of the IOMMU MMIO. The range is mapped in the hypervisor
private range and annotated as owned by the hypervisor in the host
stage-2, so host accesses fault and further donations of the same range
are refused. MMIO has no struct hyp_page, so the annotation is the only
state, and a dedicated function is simpler than teaching the memory
donation path about MMIO. Donation is only allowed before the host is
de-privileged, which is when the hypervisor IOMMU drivers initialise.

Make kvm_pgtable_stage2_unmap() leave invalid counted PTEs (annotations)
in place. host_stage2_unmap_dev_all() unmaps all non-memory ranges to
recycle page-table pages under memory pressure, and would otherwise drop
the annotation and let the host fault the MMIO back in. No caller depends
on the old behaviour: host_stage2_unmap_dev_all() only covers non-memory
ranges, where no annotation existed until now, and the guest unmap paths
check that the PTE is valid first. Returning an error for counted PTEs
instead is not an option, as a non-zero visitor return stops the walk,
which would abort recycling at the first hypervisor-owned MMIO page and
inject an abort into the host for an unrelated address.

Link: https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261015200903.2735565-2-smostafa@google.com
Signed-off-by: Mostafa Saleh <smostafa@google.com>
[Fuad: pass kvm_pgtable_get_leaf() the walk flags ee26443f3dd70 added;
 drop __pkvm_hyp_donate_host_mmio(), which this series doesn't call]
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v2: the version from Mostafa's SMMUv3 v9, which maps
    the MMIO in the hypervisor's private range rather than at
    __hyp_va().

[PATCH v3 00/13] KVM: ITS hardening for pKVM
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Hi folks,

Changes since v2 [1]:
  - Redesigned: the takeover, the register handling, the command checks,
    and how the tables and ITTs are taken. The new design is described
    below, and each patch from v2 lists its changes below its ---.
  - Restructured the patches, merging and redoing some of them.

This is v3 of the ITS hardening series [1], which I'm carrying while
Seb is on leave. Much of the design and the code were redone, so it's
described whole here.

The ITS writes to the memory its command queue and tables point to, and
the host owns all of it, so host control of the ITS is a write
primitive into hypervisor and protected-VM memory.

When the hypervisor initialises, it takes ownership of each ITS's
control frame. Inside the stop_machine() that deprivileges the host, it
takes the command queue and tables the ITS registers point to, and the
ITTs of the devices already mapped. If that fails, no CPU is
deprivileged and KVM_CREATE_VM fails.

From then on, every host access to the control frame traps
(GITS_TRANSLATER stays host-mapped). The hypervisor checks each command
before copying it into the hardware queue, takes each ITT for as long
as its device is mapped, and writes each level-1 device table entry
only once. A command or register write it rejects aborts the host's
access.

This is the ITS part. The redistributor part follows as its own series.

A few points for discussion:

  - GICv4 is off for the host in protected mode (patch 3): the vLPI
    commands aren't emulated yet, and VMAPP's VPT_base is a host-chosen
    address the ITS and the redistributors write to. The host loses
    direct injection for its non-protected guests. The ITS part stands
    on its own, and what the host gives up is a feature that is off by
    default (kvm-arm.vgic_v4_enable).

  - The hypervisor's device tracker lives in the eight pages the driver
    gives it at setup, which hold about a thousand devices per ITS on a
    4K host. Past that, its_create_device() fails as it would on running
    out of LPIs. I've left growing it for when a platform needs more.

  - KVM doesn't initialise in protected mode on a host with more than
    eight ITSes, as each ITS takes one of the eight MMIO region slots
    (patch 9).

Tested on QEMU with a reproducer that writes to the ITS through /dev/mem
from the host's userspace, as a hostile host would. The hypervisor
rejects forged level-1 entries, out-of-range MAPTIs, MAPTIs queued after
their device's unmap and the register writes it doesn't allow, and
still forwards the commands queued before a rejected one.

A PCIe device hot-added after deprivilege, then unbound and rebound,
exercises publishing a level-1 entry and taking and releasing the ITT,
and is clean under PROVE_LOCKING. Hosts with 16K or 64K pages, or with
GICv3 and no GICv4, boot and run a non-protected guest, and
vgic_lpi_stress passes with and without pKVM.

In a debug build that caps the tracker, the driver fails the extra
device. In one that forces the takeover to fail, the host boots and
KVM_CREATE_VM fails. Not tested: hardware, two ITSes, and suspend and
resume (QEMU's PSCI has no SYSTEM_SUSPEND).

The series is structured as follows:

  01:     MMIO donation.
  02:     Taking host-registered MMIO regions at finalisation.
  03-06:  Driver preparation: GICv4 off, page-aligned ITTs, the wait
          target under the ITS lock, the shared command accessors.
  07:     Deprivileging the host under stop_machine().
  08-09:  The takeover: the driver's entry points, then the hypervisor
          trapping the control frame, taking the command queue and
          emulating GITS_CTLR and GITS_CBASER.
  10-11:  The tables and GITS_BASER<n>, then the ITTs.
  12:     Bounding the IDs that select what the ITS writes.
  13:     Documentation.

Based on v7.4-rc1.

Cheers,
/fuad

[1] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260807164322.2970811-2-sebastianene@google.com/

  irqchip/gic-v3-its: Page-align ITT allocations under protected KVM

[PATCH v5] KVM: arm64: Trap guest MPAM accesses whenever MPAM is implemented
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

finalise_el2_state() clears the EL2 MPAM traps on every CPU whose ID
registers advertise MPAM once the arm64.nompam override is applied.
KVM sets them again on guest entry only when ARM64_MPAM is set, a
capability that also requires MPAM1_EL1.MPAMEN. Without EL3, only
MPAM2_EL2.MPAMEN is writable, and the kernel never sets it, so
ARM64_MPAM stays clear and so do the traps. A guest can then access
MPAM0_EL1, MPAM1_EL1, MPAMSM_EL1 and MPAMIDR_EL1 while its
ID_AA64PFR0_EL1.MPAM reads 0.

Record two per-CPU flags at KVM's CPU init: whether MPAM is
implemented as finalise_el2_state() tests it, and whether
MPAMIDR_EL1.HAS_HCR is set (MPAMHCR_EL2 is UNDEFINED without it).
Read ID_AA64PFR0_EL1, ID_AA64PFR1_EL1 and MPAMIDR_EL1 from cpu_data,
which holds them with the overrides applied since commit 342e62ded77c6
("arm64: cpufeature: Read MPAMIDR_EL1 in __cpuinfo_store_cpu()"). Set
the traps from those flags. MPAMEN isn't part of any MPAM trap
condition, so the traps take effect whether or not it is set.

Fixes: 31ff96c38ea3 ("KVM: arm64: Fix missing traps of guest accesses to the MPAM registers")
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v4:
     - Take ID_AA64PFR0_EL1, ID_AA64PFR1_EL1 and MPAMIDR_EL1 from cpu_data
       (was __read_sysreg_by_encoding() and read_sysreg_s()).
     - Commit message tightened, and the TIDR comment no longer
       rewritten.
     - Ben's Reviewed-by dropped, since the probe changed.
     - Rebased on 7.4-rc1.
    
    Based on Linux 7.4-rc1 (6628e7d4c6b25).

[PATCH] KVM: arm64: Don't livelock when AT can't resolve a fault IPA
Author: Ard Biesheuvel <ardb+git@google.com>


When a stage-2 permission fault doesn't report its IPA in HPFAR_EL2,
__get_fault_info() recovers it with an AT S1E1R/S1E1A on FAR_EL2. If that
fails, kvm_hyp_handle_memory_fault() goes straight back to the guest, on
the assumption that the replayed access will either succeed or take a
fault that the slow path can handle.

That assumption doesn't hold when the AT fails because its stage-1 walk
takes a stage-2 fault on one of the guest's page-table pages, while the
guest still holds a cached translation for the faulting VA. The guest
doesn't walk its page tables, so it never takes the S1PTW fault that
would let the slow path fix things up. It takes the same permission fault
again, the AT fails again, and the vCPU livelocks in the world switch.

This was observed on Apple M2, which has no hardware Access flag
management at stage 2, and no DIC, so execute permission is granted
lazily and the first instruction fetch from a page takes a permission
fault. With DAMON_STAT enabled (CONFIG_DAMON_STAT_ENABLED_DEFAULT=y),
kdamond keeps sampling guest memory, and damon_pmdp_mkold() ages a whole
PMD at a time through mmu_notifier_clear_young(). With THP-backed guest
memory, kvm_age_gfn() therefore clears the stage-2 AF of 32MB of guest
pages at once, without TLB invalidation. When that covers both freshly
loaded code and the page-table page that maps it, the first instruction
fetch from that code livelocks, and the vCPU only ever recovers when its
thread happens to be moved to another CPU. In all 52 instrumented
occurrences, the AT reported a stage-2 Access flag fault on a stage-1
table walk (PAR_EL1.{S,PTW} = {1,1}, FST = Access flag fault, level 3),
while an AT S12E1R on the same VA succeeded. With DAMON_STAT disabled,
the AT didn't fail once in 210 boots. Nothing in this is specific to
nested virtualisation, nor to instruction fetches: a write to a
read-only memslot hit it too.

A stage-1 fault can lead to the same livelock. A guest that ages its own
page tables without invalidating the TLB, as MGLRU does through
ptep_test_and_clear_young(), can keep using a cached translation while
the descriptor in memory has its Access flag clear. If such an access
takes a stage-2 permission fault (lazy execute permission, or dirty
logging), the AT reports a stage-1 Access flag fault (PAR_EL1.S = 0),
which the guest never takes either, as it doesn't walk its page tables.
This hasn't been observed, but it is why the invalidation below is done
whenever the AT fails, rather than only when PAR_EL1.PTW reports a
stage-2 fault on the walk. A guest that merely races with its own
page-table updates isn't harmed by it: at worst, it takes a translation
fault that it was allowed to take anyway.

Invalidate this VMID's TLB entries on the local CPU before replaying the
access, so that the guest has to walk its page tables and takes the
fault that the AT ran into: either a stage-2 fault on the page-table
page, with a valid HPFAR_EL2, which the slow path resolves, or a stage-1
fault, which the guest resolves itself. In all instrumented cases, the
next exit was an S1PTW Access flag fault on the page-table page. This
only happens when the AT fails, so the cost is negligible.

With this change, an EL2 UEFI guest that livelocked in 27 out of 210
boots didn't hang once in 420 boots.

Signed-off-by: Ard Biesheuvel <ardb@kernel.org>
---
Cc: Marc Zyngier <maz@kernel.org>
Cc: Oliver Upton <oupton@kernel.org>
Cc: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
Cc: Joey Gouly <joey.gouly@arm.com>
Cc: Steffen Eiden <seiden@linux.ibm.com>
Cc: Suzuki K Poulose <suzuki.poulose@arm.com>
Cc: Zenghui Yu <yuzenghui@huawei.com>

[PATCH] KVM: arm64: Add support for self-hosted debug in protected guests
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

pKVM hides DebugVer from protected guests and makes their debug
registers RAZ/WI or UNDEFINED, so they don't have breakpoints,
watchpoints or single-step. For a non-protected guest, the host sets the
owner of the debug registers at vcpu load, from the guest's MDSCR_EL1
and OS Lock, and EL2 copies the owner and the registers in at each
entry. However, the host can't see a protected guest's registers.

Set the owner at EL2 for a protected vCPU instead, by the rules the host
uses for a non-protected guest, but keep MDCR_EL2.TDA set when the guest
is the owner. A trap handler returns to the guest without going back
through the world switch, so a handler that changes the owner switches
the debug registers itself. The PSCI CPU_ON reset runs after the vcpu
load, so it sets the owner again.

Advertise DebugVer, capped at V8P8 as for a non-protected guest, and the
breakpoint, watchpoint and context-aware breakpoint counts. The other
debug registers that took an UNDEF behave as they do for a non-protected
guest that doesn't own its debug registers.

Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Commit 3a81a814437d ("KVM: arm64: Expose self-hosted debug regs as
    RAZ/WI for protected guests") made these registers RAZ/WI and left
    debug support for later.
    
    Tested by running the breakpoint_test_arm64 kselftest in a protected
    guest under kvmtool. It passes on hVHE and nVHE hosts under QEMU,
    and on hVHE again after offlining and onlining the guest CPU it runs
    on. That test has no single-step case, so I checked single-step with
    the pkvm_exceptions test from my pKVM selftests series, coming soon.
    
    Based on Linux 7.4-rc1 (6628e7d4c6b25).

[PATCH v2] KVM: arm64: vgic-its: Update GITS_CTLR.Enabled under its_lock
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

A GITS_CTLR write that disables the ITS clears its->enabled and
invalidates the translation cache under cmd_lock, while an MSI
injection tests the enable bit and populates the cache under its_lock.
An injection that passed the test before the disable can cache its
translation after the invalidation, and the cached path then injects
the LPI from the disabled ITS on every later MSI for that event.

Take its_lock around the store and the invalidation, so that it
serializes the store against the test. Nest it under cmd_lock, as the
command handlers do.

Fixes: 86a7dae884f38 ("KVM: arm/arm64: vgic-its: Check the LPI translation cache on MSI injection")
Reported-by: Hyunwoo Kim <imv4bel@gmail.com>
Link: https://lore.kernel.org/ah6Lsi4MfKUU6wBR@v4bel/
Suggested-by: Oliver Upton <oupton@kernel.org>
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

Notes:
    Changes since v1 [1]:
      - Reworded the commit message.
      - Rebased on 7.4-rc1.
    
    Picking up the fix Marc was worried had stalled [2].
    
    The GICR_CTLR.EnableLPIs path has the same window and is left as
    is. vgic_its_invalidate_all_caches() can't take kvm->lock there (it
    runs with vcpu->mutex held) or config_lock (the userspace GICR_CTLR
    route already holds it). Instead, the vgic_target_oracle() check in
    my LPIs-disabled series [3] keeps an LPI off a redistributor with
    EnableLPIs clear. The two don't depend on each other.
    
    vgic_its_free_collection_list() still leaves the cache alone. A
    guest reaches it only through a GITS_BASER write, which already
    drops the cache (8cd92f77ae4f). An ITS reset frees the devices
    first, which drops it too. That leaves a failed collection-table
    restore. A VMM gets there with translations cached only by enabling
    the ITS before the restore, against the documented order. Those
    translations then keep injecting their LPIs into the old target
    vCPUs.
    
    Based on Linux 7.4-rc1 (6628e7d4c6b25; also applies to kvmarm/fixes
    79f3724ca8913).
    
    [1] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260917162020.1612577-1-fuad.tabba@linux.dev/
    [2] https://lore.kernel.org/861pbr6emw.wl-maz@kernel.org/
    [3] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261026092802.2636798-1-fuad.tabba@linux.dev/

[PATCH v4 2/2] KVM: arm64: Clear the default PMU when kvm_setup_vcpu() fails
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Under KVM_ARM_VCPU_PMU_V3_STRICT, the VM has no PMU until userspace
selects one, and initializing the PMU fails with -ENXIO until then.
However, without STRICT, kvm_setup_vcpu() selects the default PMU
before it prepares the vCPU for nested virtualization, and leaves it
set when that preparation fails. After a retry with STRICT,
initializing the PMU succeeds and the guest gets the PMU of the CPU
the failed INIT ran on.

Drop the PMU and its number of counters along with the VM's feature
bitmap. While KVM_ARCH_FLAG_VCPU_FEATURES_CONFIGURED is clear, no vCPU
has been initialized and the PMU attributes return -ENODEV, so only
such a failure can have set them. Once the flag is set, every INIT has the
features of the first one that succeeded, which either selected a PMU
or had none to select, so kvm_setup_vcpu() doesn't select another.

Fixes: ad220e275c398 ("KVM: arm64: Add KVM_ARM_VCPU_PMU_V3_STRICT vCPU feature")
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---
 arch/arm64/kvm/arm.c | 7 +++++--
 1 file changed, 5 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)

[PATCH v4 1/2] KVM: arm64: Clear the VM's feature bitmap when kvm_setup_vcpu() fails
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT records the requested features in the VM-wide bitmap
before kvm_setup_vcpu() runs, and leaves them there when setup fails
(e.g., HAS_EL2_E2H0 on a host without FEAT_NV1). The PMU attributes
read that bitmap before the vCPU is initialized, so after such a
failure with PMU_V3 requested, KVM_SET_DEVICE_ATTR on the PMU group no
longer returns -ENODEV.

Until an INIT has succeeded, every INIT that gets past the feature
checks rewrites the bitmap. After that, kvm_vcpu_init_changed()
requires the requested features to match it, so the copy is a no-op.
Clear the bitmap on failure while KVM_ARCH_FLAG_VCPU_FEATURES_CONFIGURED
is clear.

Fixes: 427733579744e ("KVM: arm64: Select default PMU in KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT handler")
Suggested-by: Oliver Upton <oupton@kernel.org>
Reviewed-by: Lorenzo Stoakes (ARM) <ljs@kernel.org>
Signed-off-by: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>
---

[PATCH v4 0/2] KVM: arm64: Clear VM-wide state when kvm_setup_vcpu() fails
Author: Fuad Tabba <fuad.tabba@linux.dev>

Hi folks,

Changes since v3 [1]:
  - New patch 2, clearing the default PMU left set by a failed INIT.
  - Reworded patch 1's commit message.
  - Rebased on 7.4-rc1.

A KVM_ARM_VCPU_INIT whose kvm_setup_vcpu() fails doesn't undo its
changes to VM-wide state. Patch 1 clears the feature bitmap, which the
PMU attributes read before the vCPU is initialized. Patch 2 clears the
default PMU, which a retry with KVM_ARM_VCPU_PMU_V3_STRICT would
otherwise inherit.

Tested under QEMU on a host without FEAT_NV1 (an Apple M2 MIDR,
for which has_nv1() returns false), with a reproducer that fails an
INIT and then initializes the PMU, directly for patch 1 and after a
STRICT retry for patch 2.

Based on Linux 7.4-rc1.

Cheers,
/fuad
